On weak Hopf symmetry and weak Hopf quantum double model
Zhian Jia, Sheng Tan, Dagomir Kaszlikowski, and Liang Chang

Abstract
Symmetry is a central concept for classical and quantum field theory, usually, symmetry is described by a finite group or Lie group. In this work, we introduce the weak Hopf algebra extension of symmetry, which arises naturally in anyonic quantum systems; and we establish weak Hopf symmetry breaking theory based on the fusion closed set of anyons. As a concrete example, we implement a thorough investigation of the quantum double model based on a given weak Hopf algebra and show that the vacuum sector of the model has weak Hopf symmetry. The topological excitations and ribbon operators are discussed in detail. The gapped boundary and domain wall theories are also established. We show that the gapped boundary is algebraically determined by a comodule algebra, or equivalently, a module algebra; and the gapped domain wall is determined by the bicomodule algebra, or equivalently, a bimodule algebra. The microscopic lattice constructions of the gapped boundary and domain wall are discussed in detail. We also introduce the weak Hopf tensor network states, via which we solve the weak Hopf quantum double lattice models on closed and open surfaces. The duality of the quantum double phases is discussed in the last part.



1
Introduction

The study of topologically ordered phases of matter has attracted a considerable amount of attention during the past several decades
Wen 2004
;
Levin and Wen 2005a
;
Nayak et al. 2008
;
Chiu et al. 2016
;
Witten 2016
;
Zhou et al. 2017
;
Wen 2017
.
These topological quantum phases of matter usually have symmetries that are beyond the Landau-Ginzburg symmetry-breaking paradigm. To understand these exotic quantum phases of matter, several kinds of generalized concepts of symmetry have been proposed: (i) higher form symmetry, for which the charged particles have a dimension
p>0
, such as string, membrane, volume, etc.
Gaiotto et al. 2015
;
(ii) non-invertible symmetry, for which the symmetry operators are in general non-invertible and form a fusion algebra
Bhardwaj and Tachikawa 2018
;
Bhardwaj et al. 2022
;
Bartsch et al. 2022
rather than a group;
(iii) categorical symmetry, where some monoidal category with extra structures characterizes the symmetry, like the unitary fusion category (UFC) and unitary modular tensor category (UMTC), and even higher category
Levin and Wen 2005b
;
Kong and Wen 2014
;
Johnson-Freyd 2022
.
These concepts of symmetry are not independent, they are closely related to each other, and many efforts have been made to subsume these symmetries in a unified framework.

Categorical symmetry is a promising candidate framework for characterizing the generalized phase transition where there is no symmetry breaking in the usual sense, like fractional quantum Hall system
Tsui et al. 1982
;
Wen 2004
. There are several different constructions of UFC symmetry in
(1+1)D
, like rational CFT
Fröhlich et al. 2007
, TQFT
Thorngren and Wang 2019
;
Thorngren and Wang 2021
;
Inamura 2021
;
Inamura 2022
, anyonic chain
Feiguin et al. 2007
;
Gils et al. 2013
;
Buican and Gromov 2017
, and so on.
The UMTC symmetry in
(2+1)D
is also extensively investigated, like Levin-Wen string-net model
Levin and Wen 2005b
, Kitaev quantum double model
Kitaev 2003
, etc.
It is well known that the representation category of a Hopf algebra is a UFC. And when the Hopf algebra is equipped with a quasi-triangular structure, its representation category is a UMTC.
This quantum group symmetry has been investigated for a long time
Majid 2000
, and it sits in between the usual group symmetry and categorical symmetry.
A comprehensive understanding of Hopf algebra symmetry may shed new light on the understanding of phase, phase transition, and categorical symmetry.

A typical Hopf algebra symmetry is the so-called quantum double symmetry.
Consider the
(2+1)D
discrete gauge theory, like Gauge-Higgs theory for which Higgs field has broken the continuous gauge group
U
down to a finite group
G

Propitius and Bais 1995
;
Alexander Bais et al. 1992
. The theory becomes topological in the low energy limit or long-distance limit.
The topological excitations carry the fusion and braiding data. These data are mathematically captured by
\mathsf{Rep}(D(G))
, the representation category of quantum double for some finite group
G
.
The Hopf algebraic generalization of the formalism is discussed in
Bais et al. 2003
, where the unbroken symmetry is given by a Hopf algebra
H
, for which group algebra is a particular case.
It turns out that this Hopf algebraic symmetry has broad applications in quantum gravity
Bais et al. 2002
;
Majid 2000
;
Delcamp et al. 2017
, conformal field theory (CFT)
Fuchs 1995
, topological quantum field theory (TQFT)
Meusburger and Wise 2021
;
Meusburger 2017
, quantum Hall effect
Slingerland and Bais 2001
, topological phase
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
;
Buerschaper et al. 2013b
;
Meusburger 2017
;
Meusburger and Wise 2021
;
Koppen 2020
;
Girelli et al. 2021
;
Voß 2021
;
Yan et al. 2022
;
Jia et al. 2022
, and so on.

From a lattice model perspective, Kitaev proposed the so-called quantum double model based on a finite group
Kitaev 2003
, which turned out to be a powerful lattice model that has potential applications in many fields, including topological quantum computation
Nayak et al. 2008
, and topological quantum error correcting code
Terhal 2015
.
The topological orders of these quantum double phases have been extensively investigated from many aspects, e.g., topological ground state degeneracy
Kitaev 2003
, anyons and their mutual statistics
Kitaev 2003
, topological entanglement entropy
Kitaev and Preskill 2006
, boundary and domain wall
Bravyi and Kitaev 1998
;
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
;
Beigi et al. 2011
;
Kitaev and Kong 2012
;
Cong et al. 2017a
, anyon condensation
Kong 2014
, and electric-magnetic (EM) duality
Buerschaper and Aguado 2009
;
Buerschaper et al. 2013b
;
Wang et al. 2020
;
Hu and Wan 2020
;
Jia and Kaszlikowski 2022
.
Its twisted generalization
Bullivant et al. 2017
and higher dimensional (twisted) generalization
Moradi and Wen 2015
;
Wan et al. 2015
;
Wang et al. 2018
;
Levin and Wen 2005b
;
Hamma et al. 2005
;
Kong et al. 2020
;
Delcamp and Schuch 2021
;
Delcamp 2022
are also discussed.
However, in a general (weak) Hopf algebra setting, such a characterization of topological properties of the quantum double model has been proved far more elusive and they are largely unexplored
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
;
Meusburger 2017
;
Meusburger and Wise 2021
;
Koppen 2020
;
Girelli et al. 2021
;
Voß 2021
;
Yan et al. 2022
;
Jia et al. 2022
,
even less research has been done on the weak Hopf quantum dual model
Chang 2014
.

On the other hand, the Hopf and weak Hopf algebras provide us with the general framework to understand symmetry and quantized space-time.
The notion of a weak Hopf algebra is proposed in
Böhm et al. 1999
. As a generalization of Hopf algebra, it is co-associative with weaker unit and counit.
They are designed in such a way that their representation category has a monoidal structure.
In fact, it is shown that any finite monoidal category can be realized as a representation category for some weak Hopf algebra
Ostrik 2003
.
Thus a comprehensive investigation of weak Hopf symmetry will be helpful for us to understand categorical symmetry.
Compared with categorical symmetry, weak Hopf symmetry is easier to control due to its intimate relation with the group description of symmetry.
This is one motivation for us to carry out the current work.
From the lattice model perspective, the weak Hopf quantum double lattice models are of their own interests.
Since both group algebra and Hopf algebra are special cases of weak Hopf algebras, establishing the theories of topological excitation, gapped boundary, gapped domain wall, and duality of weak Hopf quantum double model can solve the corresponding problems for quantum double phase once and for all.

In this work, we take the first step to understanding the weak Hopf symmetry and weak Hopf quantum double model.
We establish the theory of weak Hopf symmetry and its breaking.
As a concrete example, we carefully investigate the weak Hopf quantum double model, including its lattice construction, topological excitation and ribbon operators, its boundary and domain wall theory, and the duality between different weak Hopf quantum double models.

Our first aim of the paper is to summarize the basic facts about weak Hopf algebras and establish the theory of weak Hopf symmetry and weak Hopf quantum double model in a way hopefully accessible to readers from high energy, condensed matter, and quantum information communities.
Thus in the first part (Sec.
2
), we collect some necessary definitions, formulae, and theorems that will be used later. We have tried to give more details whenever we think it is not too lengthy to explain.

Sec.
3
establishes the theory of weak Hopf symmetry and its breaking.
Our discussion of symmetry focuses on the symmetry of the vacuum sector.
Using the concept of fusion closed set of anyons
Bais et al. 2003
and injective and surjective morphisms of weak Hopf algebras, we obtain our main result in this part, Theorems
1
and
2
.

Sec.
4
is devoted to the discussion of the weak Hopf quantum double lattice model.
The construction has been briefly discussed by one of the authors in
Chang 2014
.
Here we give a more detailed discussion, including the local stabilizer, the ribbon operators, and the topological excitations.

Sec.
5
and
6
generalize our algebraic theory and lattice construction of gapped boundary and domain wall
Jia et al. 2022
of Hopf quantum double model to the weak Hopf quantum double lattice.
We show that most results remain similar to the Hopf case.

Sec.
7
solves the weak Hopf quantum double model using the weak Hopf tensor network representation of states.

Sec.
8
discusses the duality of weak Hopf quantum double models.
The topological phases related by a duality can be regarded as the boundary theories of a one-dimension higher topological order.
For the Hopf algebra case, the duality is fully characterized by the twist deformation.

In Sec.
9
, we conclude and discuss future questions. In the appendix, we collect some detailed calculations about weak Hopf quantum double and ribbon operators.

2
Preliminary

To start with, let us briefly review some basic concepts and mathematical results concerning weak Hopf algebra (a.k.a. quantum groupoid) and meanwhile fix our notations.
Hereinafter, we assume all algebras are finite-dimensional unless otherwise specified.
The reader familiar with the notion of weak Hopf algebra might skip this part and get back if necessary.

Definition 1

(Weak bialgebra
Böhm and Szlachónyi 1996
;
Böhm et al. 1999
;
Nill 1998
)
.

A complex weak bialgebra
(W,\mu,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
W
equipped with the following structures:

•

An algebra structure
(W,\mu,\eta)
, where
\mu:W\otimes W\to W
and
\eta:\mathbb{C}\to W
are linear morphisms called multiplication and unit morphisms respectively. Diagrammatically,

(2.3)

We can abbreviate
\mu(a,b)=a\cdot b
, and the unit element is denoted as
1_{W}=\eta(1)
.

•

A coalgebra structure
(W,\Delta,\varepsilon)
, where
\Delta:W\to W\otimes W
and
\varepsilon:W\to\mathbb{C}
are linear morphisms called comultiplication and counit morphisms respectively. Diagrammatically,

\Delta=\begin{aligned} \hbox to29.45pt{\vbox to36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.72638pt\lower-7.044pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.69 39.37 C -15.75 0 15.75 0 19.69 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(2.4)

They satisfy

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta,\quad(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=\mathrm{id}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta.

(2.6)

We will adopt the Sweedler’s notation
\Delta(u)=\sum_{(u)}u^{(1)}\otimes u^{(2)}:=\sum_{i}u_{i}^{(1)}\otimes u_{i}^{(2)}
.
The comultiplication law ensures that
(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta(u)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta(u)=\sum_{(u)}u^{(1)}\otimes u^{(2)}\otimes u^{(3)}
. In general, we define
\Delta_{1}=\Delta
and
\Delta_{n}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{n-1}
, then
\Delta_{n}(u)=\sum_{(u)}u^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes u^{(n+1)}
.

To form a weak bialgebra, the above algebra and coalgebra structures must satisfy the following consistency conditions:

(1)

The comultiplication preserves multiplication, i.e.,
\Delta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\mu=\mu_{W\otimes W}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\Delta\otimes\Delta)=(\mu\otimes\mu)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\sigma_{W,W}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\Delta\otimes\Delta)
,

(2.11)

\varepsilon(uvw)=\sum_{(v)}\varepsilon(uv^{(1)})\varepsilon(v^{(2)}w)=\sum_{(v)}\varepsilon(uv^{(2)})\varepsilon(v^{(1)}w),\quad\forall\,u,v,w\in W.

(2.12)

Definition 2

(Weak Hopf algebra
Böhm and Szlachónyi 1996
;
Böhm et al. 1999
;
Nill 1998
)
.

A complex weak Hopf algebra
(W,\mu,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
is a weak bialgebra
(W,\mu,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon)
equipped with a linear morphism
S:W\to W
called antipode which satisfies the following three conditions:

(1)

\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes S)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes\sigma_{W,W})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\eta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})
,

(2.13)

The map on the right hand side will be denoted as
\varepsilon_{L}:W\to W
,
\varepsilon_{L}(h)=(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta(1_{W})(h\otimes 1_{W}))=\sum_{(1_{W})}\varepsilon(1_{W}^{(1)}h)1^{(2)}_{W}
and we denote
W_{L}=\varepsilon_{L}(W)
.
Equivalently, we have

\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta(h)=\varepsilon_{L}(h),\quad\forall\,h\in W.

(2.14)

(2)

\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(S\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes\varepsilon)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes\mu)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\sigma_{W,W}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes\eta)

The map on the right hand side will be denoted as

,

.

(2.17)

Equivalently, we have

S(h)=\sum_{(h)}S(h^{(1)})h^{(2)}S(h^{(3)}),\quad\forall\,h\in W.

(2.18)

A Hopf algebra is a particular case of weak Hopf algebra.
If
\Delta(1_{W})=1_{W}\otimes 1_{W}
, then
\Delta
is an algebra homomorphism. From Eq. (
2.12
) by taking
v=1_{W}
, we see that
\varepsilon
is also an algebra homomorphism. Eqs. (
2.14
) and (
2.16
) imply the consistency condition of the antipode for Hopf algebra. In this case,
W
becomes a Hopf algebra.
Also, if
\varepsilon
is a homomorphism of algebras, acting on the middle top of Eq. (
2.9
)
with
\varepsilon
, from graphical calculus, it is easily checked that
\Delta(1_{W})=1_{W}\otimes 1_{W}
, so
W
becomes a Hopf algebra. If
S
is a Hopf antipode, then
W
also becomes a Hopf algebra
Nill 1998
;
Böhm et al. 1999
.

Notice that for a weak Hopf algebra, the antipode is anti-multiplicative and anti-comultiplicative

S(xy)=S(y)S(x),\quad\sum_{(S(x))}S(x)^{(1)}\otimes S(x)^{(2)}=\sum_{(x)}S(x^{(2)})\otimes S(x^{(1)}).

(2.19)

The antipode preserves the unit and the counit

S(1_{W})=1_{W},\quad\varepsilon\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S=\varepsilon.

(2.20)

And the antipode
S
is invertible. See
(
Böhm et al. 1999
, Theorem 2.10)
for proofs of the above properties.
Note that in contrast to finite-dimensional semisimple Hopf algebras where
S^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, the antipode in a finite-dimensional weak Hopf algebra is not necessarily involutive
Nikshych et al. 2003
.
If
W
is a weak Hopf algebra such that
S^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, it is called a weak Kac algebra.

The
W_{L}=\varepsilon_{L}(W),W_{R}=\varepsilon_{R}(W)
in Definition
2
are two separable subalgebras of
W

1
1

1

When
W
is a Hopf algebra,
\varepsilon_{L}=\varepsilon_{R}:x\mapsto\varepsilon(x)1_{W}
.
. They are called left and right counital subalgebras and play crucial roles in studying weak Hopf symmetry.
We have
\varepsilon_{L}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S=\varepsilon_{L}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\varepsilon_{R}=S\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\varepsilon_{R}
and
\varepsilon_{R}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S=\varepsilon_{R}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\varepsilon_{L}=S\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\varepsilon_{L}
. It holds that
\Delta(1_{W})\in W_{R}\otimes W_{L}
.

A complex weak Hopf algebra
W
is called simple (or indecomposable) if its underlying algebra
(W,\mu,\eta)
has no nontrivial subalgebras, and is called semisimple if its underlying algebra can be written as a direct sum of simple algebras.
A
*
-weak Hopf algebra
(W,*)
is a weak Hopf algebra
W
equipped with a
C^{*}
-structure
*:W\to W
such that
\Delta
is a
*
-homomorphism. That is

(x^{*})^{*}=x,\;(x+y)^{*}=x^{*}+y^{*},\;(xy)^{*}=y^{*}x^{*},\;(cx)^{*}=\bar{c}x^{*},\;\Delta(x)^{*}=\Delta(x^{*}),

(2.21)

for all
x,y\in W
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
. We also have
S(x^{*})=S^{-1}(x)^{*}
.

(W,*)
is called a
C^{*}
-weak Hopf algebra if there exists a fully faithful
*
-representation
\rho:W\to\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{H})
for some operator space over some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

Let
W
be a weak Hopf algebra. A left (resp. right) integral of
W
is an element
l
(resp.
r
) satisfying
xl=\varepsilon_{L}(x)l
(resp.
rx=r\varepsilon_{R}(x)
).
A left (resp. right) integral
l
(resp.
r
) is called left (resp. right) normalized if
\varepsilon_{L}(l)=1_{W}
(resp.
\varepsilon_{R}(r)=1_{W}
).
If
h
is both a left and right integral, it is called a two-side integral.
A Haar integral in
W
(or Haar measure on
\hat{W}
) is a two-side normalized two-side integral.

Notice that Haar integral in a weak Hopf algebra, if exists, must be unique. To see this, suppose that
h,h^{\prime}
are two Haar integrals, then
h^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{L}(h)h^{\prime}=hh^{\prime}=h\varepsilon_{R}(h^{\prime})=h
. Since
S(h)
is a Haar integral if
h
is, then from uniqueness we see that Haar integral is
S
-invariant,
S(h)=h
. It is also clear that
h^{2}=\varepsilon_{L}(h)h=h
.
A
C^{*}
weak Hopf algebra always has a unique Haar integral
h
satisfying
h^{*}=h

Böhm et al. 1999
.
An element
h\in W
is called cocommutative if
\Delta^{\rm op}(h)=\Delta(h)
; the set of all cocommutative elements in
W
is denoted as
\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
. We will only consider weak Hopf algebras which are
C^{*}
and whose Haar integral is cocommutative.

A crucial tool that we will use is the pairing between two weak Hopf algebras.
A pairing
\lambda=\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:J\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
between two weak Hopf algebras
J,K
is a bilinear map satisfying

\displaystyle\langle hg,a\rangle=\sum_{(a)}\langle h,a^{(1)}\rangle\langle g,a^{(2)}\rangle,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\langle h,ab\rangle=\sum_{(h)}\langle h^{(1)},a\rangle\langle h^{(2)},b\rangle,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\langle 1_{J},a\rangle=\varepsilon_{K}(a),\quad\langle h,1_{K}\rangle=\varepsilon_{J}(h).

(2.24)

A pairing between
J^{\rm cop}
(the weak Hopf algebra with coproduct
\Delta^{\rm op}
, see below) and
K
is called a skew-pairing between
J
and
K
.

For a
C^{*}
weak Hopf algebra
W
, its dual space
\hat{W}:=\mathrm{Hom}(W,\mathbb{C})=W^{\vee}
has a canonical
C^{*}
weak Hopf algebra structure induced by the canonical pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:W^{\vee}\times W\to\mathbb{C}
,
\langle\varphi,h\rangle:=\varphi(h)
. More precisely,
\hat{\mu}=\Delta^{\vee}
,
\hat{\eta}=\varepsilon^{\vee}
,
\hat{\Delta}=\mu^{\vee}
,
\hat{\varepsilon}=\eta^{\vee}
and
\hat{S}=S^{\vee}
,
viz.
,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mu}(\varphi\otimes\psi),x\rangle=\langle\varphi\otimes\psi,\Delta(x)\rangle,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\eta}(1),x\rangle=\varepsilon(x),\;\text{i.e.,}\;\hat{1}=\varepsilon,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\Delta}(\varphi),x\otimes y\rangle=\langle\varphi,\mu(x\otimes y)\rangle,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\langle\varphi,\eta(1)\rangle,

(2.28)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{S}(\varphi),x\rangle=\langle\varphi,S(x)\rangle.

(2.29)

The star operation on
\hat{W}
is defined as

\langle\varphi^{*},x\rangle=\overline{\langle\varphi,S(x)^{*}\rangle}.

(2.30)

The opposite weak Hopf algebra
W^{\rm op}
is defined as
(W,\mu^{\rm op},\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S^{-1})
, and the co-opposite weak Hopf algebra
W^{\rm cop}
is defined as
(W,\mu,\eta,\Delta^{\rm op},\varepsilon,S^{-1})
. It is easily checked that
(W^{\rm op})^{\vee}\simeq(W^{\vee})^{\rm cop}
and
(W^{\rm cop})^{\vee}\simeq(W^{\vee})^{\rm op}
as weak Hopf algebras.

When considering the action of
W
on
\hat{W}
, the Sweedler’s arrow notation will be useful,

x\rightharpoonup\varphi:=\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi^{(1)}\langle\varphi^{(2)},x\rangle,\quad\varphi\leftharpoonup x:=\sum_{(\varphi)}\langle\varphi^{(1)},x\rangle\varphi^{(2)}.

(2.31)

Since
W
is the dual weak Hopf algebra of
\hat{W}
, we also have

\varphi\rightharpoonup x:=\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\langle\varphi,x^{(2)}\rangle,\quad x\leftharpoonup\varphi:=\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,x^{(1)}\rangle x^{(2)}.

(2.32)

The left (resp. right) action of
W
on
W
will be denoted as
h\triangleright x:=hx
(resp.
x\triangleleft h=xh
), and similarly for
\hat{W}
.

The following formulae are useful for computation.
For
x_{L}=\varepsilon_{L}(x)\in W_{L},~y_{R}=\varepsilon_{R}(y)\in W_{R}
and
\varphi\in\hat{W}
, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\varphi\leftharpoonup y_{R}=\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y_{R}),\quad y_{R}\rightharpoonup\varphi=\varphi(y_{R}\rightharpoonup\varepsilon),

(2.33)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\leftharpoonup S(x_{L})=\varepsilon\leftharpoonup x_{L},\quad S(y_{R})\rightharpoonup\varepsilon=y_{R}\rightharpoonup\varepsilon,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leftharpoonup S^{-1}(y_{R})=\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y_{R},\quad S^{-1}(x_{L})\rightharpoonup\varepsilon=x_{L}\rightharpoonup\varepsilon.

(2.35)

The first line is from
(
Böhm et al. 1999
, Scholium 2.7)
. To prove the second line, for any
y\in W
,

\displaystyle\langle\varepsilon\leftharpoonup S(x_{L}),y\rangle

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(S(x_{L})y)=\varepsilon(S(\varepsilon_{L}(x))y)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\varepsilon_{R}(S(x))y)=\varepsilon(\varepsilon_{R}(\varepsilon_{L}(x))y)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(x_{L}y)=\langle\varepsilon\leftharpoonup x_{L},y\rangle.

Since this is true for any
y\in W
, it follows that
\varepsilon\leftharpoonup S(x_{L})=\varepsilon\leftharpoonup x_{L}
. The other identity in the second line is proved similar. The third lines follow from the second lines by taking
x^{\prime}_{L}=S^{-1}(y_{R})
and
y^{\prime}_{R}=S^{-1}(x_{L})
.

The Haar integral of
\hat{W}
is called the Haar measure of
W
. It is proved in
Böhm et al. 1999
that the Haar measure induces an inner product structure over
W
by

\langle x,y\rangle=\varphi_{\hat{W}}(x^{*}y).

(2.36)

Hereinafter, we will denote the Haar integrals of
W
and
\hat{W}
as
h_{W}
and
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
respectively.

In this work, we will mainly consider the lattice model whose weak Hopf symmetry is given by the quantum double of some weak Hopf algebra
W
. We shall call such a kind of lattice model a weak Hopf quantum double model.
By definition, the quantum double of
W
is a quotient algebra of
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
equipped with a weak Hopf algebra structure; we denote it as
D(W)
, and the elements are equivalence classes
[\varphi\otimes h]
.
2
2

2

There are several different constructions of quantum double
Majid 2000
. To keep the construction consistent with that for the Hopf algebra case discussed in our previous work
Jia et al. 2022
, we choose the construction here. The generalization of results in this work to all other constructions is straightforward.

Both
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}
and
W
can be embedded into
D(W)
as weak Hopf subalgebras.

For a weak Hopf algebra
W
,
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
is an algebra with the multiplication
Drinfel’d 1988
;
Majid 1990
;
Majid 1994

(\varphi\otimes h)(\psi\otimes g)=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(\psi)}\varphi\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}g\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle,

(2.37)

and the unit
\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}
. Here the comultiplication of
\psi
is taken in
\hat{W}
. The linear span
J
of the elements

\displaystyle\varphi\otimes xh-\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h,\quad x\in W_{L},

(2.38)

\displaystyle\varphi\otimes yh-\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h,\quad y\in W_{R},

(2.39)

is a two-sided ideal of
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
(see Appendix
A
). We denote the quotient algebra
(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)/J
as
D(W)
and equivalent classes in
D(W)
as
[\varphi\otimes h]
for
\varphi\otimes h\in\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
.

Definition 3

(Quantum double)
.

The quantum double of
W
is
D(W)
equipped with the following weak Hopf algebra structure:

(1)

The multiplication
[\varphi\otimes h][\psi\otimes g]=\sum_{(\psi),(h)}[\varphi\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}g]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle
.

(2)

The unit
[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}]
.

(3)

The comultiplication
\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]
.

(4)

The counit
\varepsilon([\varphi\otimes h])=\langle\varphi,\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h))\rangle
.

(5)

The antipode
S([\varphi\otimes h])=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(1)},h^{(3)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle
.

It can be verified
D(W)
is indeed a weak Hopf algebra (see Appendix
A
); see also
Nikshych et al. 2003
for other construction. We will also use the notation
D(W)=\hat{W}\Join W
, and “
\Join
” is usually called the bicrossed product.

Both
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}
and
W
are weak Hopf subalgebras of
D(W)
with inclusion maps

\displaystyle i_{\hat{W}^{\rm cop}}:\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\hookrightarrow D(W),\quad\varphi\mapsto[\varphi\otimes 1_{W}],

(2.40)

\displaystyle i_{W}:W\hookrightarrow D(W),\quad h\mapsto[\varepsilon\otimes h].

(2.41)

This is the origin of the name “quantum double”. Also, these inclusions give us the identifications
W\simeq(\varepsilon\otimes W)/J
and
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\simeq(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes 1_{W})/J
. The multiplication in
D(W)
implies that

\displaystyle i_{\hat{W}^{\rm cop}}(\varphi)i_{W}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}[\varphi\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}h]\langle\varepsilon^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varepsilon^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}[\varphi\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes\langle\varepsilon^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle 1_{W}^{(2)}1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\langle\varepsilon^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(2)})\rangle h]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}[\varphi\varepsilon^{(2)}(1_{W}^{(2)}\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\langle\varepsilon^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup 1_{W}^{\prime(1)})\langle\varepsilon^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(2)})\rangle\otimes h]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varepsilon)}[\varphi\varepsilon^{(2)}\varepsilon_{R}(\varepsilon^{(3)})S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varepsilon^{(1)}))\otimes h]=[\varphi\otimes h],

(2.42)

for all
\varphi\in\hat{W}^{\rm cop}
and
h\in W
.
3
3

3

In general,
[\varphi\otimes 1_{W}][\psi\otimes h]=[\varphi\psi\otimes h]
for any
\varphi,\psi\in\hat{W}
and
h\in W
. This can be proved exactly as Eq. (
2.42
).
Write
\varphi h
instead of
i_{\hat{W}^{\rm cop}}(\varphi)i_{W}(h)
for simplicity. Then the multiplication in
D(W)
is determined by the following “straightening formula”

h\varphi=\sum_{(h)}\varphi(S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\bullet h^{(1)})h^{(2)},\quad\forall\,\varphi\in\hat{W}^{\rm cop},\,\forall\,h\in W,

(2.43)

where “
\bullet
” is the argument of the function. For cocommutative
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
and
\varphi\in\operatorname{Cocom}(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})
, we have

[\varphi\otimes 1][\varepsilon\otimes h]=[\varphi\otimes h]=[\varepsilon\otimes h][\varphi\otimes 1].

(2.44)

Definition 4

(Yetter-Drinfeld module)
.

A left-right Yetter-Drinfeld module over
W
is a complex vector space
M
such that:

(1)

M
is a left
W
-module with the action
W\otimes M\to M
,
h\otimes m\mapsto h\triangleright m
;

(2)

M
is a right
W
-comodule with the coaction
\rho:M\to M\otimes W
,
\rho(m)=\sum_{(m)}m^{[0]}\otimes m^{[1]}
;

(3)

The action and coaction are compatible: for all
h\in W
and
m\in M
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(h),(m)}(h^{(1)}\triangleright m^{[0]})\otimes h^{(2)}m^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}(h^{(2)}\triangleright m)^{[0]}\otimes(h^{(2)}\triangleright m)^{[1]}h^{(1)},

\displaystyle\sum_{(1_{W}),(m)}(1_{W}^{(1)}\triangleright m^{[0]})\otimes

\displaystyle 1_{W}^{(2)}m^{[1]}=\sum_{(m)}m^{[0]}\otimes m^{[1]}.

Let
{{}_{W}}\mathsf{YD}^{W}
be the category of left-right Yetter-Drinfeld modules over
W
whose morphisms are
W
-linear and
W
-colinear. It is proved in
Nenciu 2002
that
{{}_{W}}\mathsf{YD}^{W}
can be identified with
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
. In fact, for
V\in\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
, it is naturally a left
W
-module and a left
\hat{W}
-module. Define the coaction
\rho:V\to V\otimes W
by
\rho(v)=\sum_{k}(\hat{k}\triangleright v)\otimes k
where
\{k\}
and
\{\hat{k}\}
are dual bases in
W
and
\hat{W}
respectively. One can verify that
V
is a left-right Yetter-Drinfeld module. Conversely, for
M\in{{}_{W}}\mathsf{YD}^{W}
, the action
[\varphi\otimes h]\triangleright m=\sum_{(m)}\varphi(m^{[1]})h\triangleright m^{[0]}
makes
M
a left
D(W)
-module. By
(
Nenciu 2002
, Theorem 4.5)
,
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(W))\simeq\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
as braided tensor categories. Its proof makes use of the aforementioned identification through
{{}_{W}}\mathsf{YD}^{W}
.

3
Weak Hopf symmetry

3.1
Weak Hopf symmetry of vacuum sector

Consider a quantum system
(H,\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS})
with Hamiltonian
H
and ground state space
\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}
, the Hamiltonian symmetry
G_{H}
is a group such that
U_{g}H=HU_{g}
for all
g\in G_{H}
, and the ground state symmetry
G_{\rm GS}
is a group such that
U_{g}
stabilizes
\mathcal{V}_{GS}
for all
g\in G_{\rm GS}
.
It is clear that
G_{H}\subseteq G_{\rm GS}
.
When dealing with quantum group symmetry, the requirement that the vacuum sector is invariant under the quantum group action is too strong, so we will need to modify the definition of symmetry
Bais et al. 2003
.
In this section, we will establish the theory of weak Hopf symmetry.

For a quantum system, we are mainly concerned about the symmetry of the vacuum sector
\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}
.
Recall that for usual group symmetry, to discuss the symmetry a phase
\mathcal{P}=\{H_{k},\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}^{k}\}
(Here, the parameter
k
serves as a label for the Hamiltonian in the phase. For example, in the case of a 1d Ising model,
k
can be chosen to represent the length of the spin chain), we need to fix a general symmetry group, usually chosen as a unitary group
4
4

4

When considering time-reversal symmetries, we also need to consider the antiunitary operators. And for the open system, the Hamiltonian is replaced with a superoperator called Lindbladian, and the symmetry operators need to be generalized to linear and antilinear completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) maps
Wei et al. 2022
.

U(\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}^{k})
for each
k
.
A
G
symmetry of the system is a group homomorphism
G\to U(\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}^{k})
such that
U_{g}
stabilizes the

\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}
for all
g\in G
. However, when dealing with Hopf algebra, this requirement is too strong.
Bais et al.
Bais et al. 2003
proposed the notion of Hopf symmetry, where a state
\psi
is called Hopf invariant if
g\triangleright\psi=\varepsilon(g)\psi
.
For the weak Hopf algebra case, we cannot straightforwardly generalize the above definition.
To see this, consider the vacuum sector
\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}
which is a
W
-module such that
g\triangleright\phi=\varepsilon_{L}(g)\triangleright\phi
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{V}_{\rm GS}
.
A general state should transform in a similar way as that of the vacuum state, this inspires us to introduce the following definition of weak Hopf symmetry:

Definition 5
.

Let
W
be a weak Hopf algebra and
\psi\in\mathcal{V}
be a state in some
W
-module
\mathcal{V}
.
We say that
\psi
is left invariant under the action of
g\in W
if
g\triangleright\psi=\varepsilon_{L}(g)\triangleright\psi
; similarly,
\psi
is right invariant under the action of
g\in W
if
\psi\triangleleft g=\psi\triangleleft\varepsilon_{R}(g)
.

If
W
is a Hopf algebra, the above definition reduces to the definition given in
Bais et al. 2003
.
If
W
is the group algebra, the group symmetry definition is also recovered.
The weak Hopf symmetry of a state
\psi
can be naturally defined as the maximal weak Hopf subalgebra that leaves
\psi
invariant in the sense of the above definition.

Definition 6
.

For a system with ground state
\psi
, the weak Hopf symmetry of the system is the maximal weak Hopf subalgebra in a weak Hopf algebra
W
that leaves
\psi
invariant.

We will denote the weak Hopf symmetry of
\psi
as
\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\psi)
.
More precisely, we define
\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\psi)
in such a way that, if weak Hopf subalgebra
K
leaves
\psi
invariant, then
K\subseteq\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\psi)
.
Notice that it is unique, because if
K,G
are two weak Hopf symmetries of
\psi
, then they can generate a larger weak Hopf symmetry that contains both of them, contradicting to the maximality of
\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\psi)
.

The basic physical data of the system are particle types, conjugation of particles, and the fusion rule between these particles.
These physical data are captured by the category of unitary left
W
-modules
\mathsf{Rep}(W)
for the weak Hopf symmetry
W
.

1.

Particle types (a.k.a., topological charges, sectors) are given by the equivalent classes of irreducible representations
a=(\rho,M_{\rho})\in\operatorname{Irr}(W)
;

2.

Antiparticles are given by the dual (conjugation) of representations
\bar{a}=(\bar{\rho},M_{\bar{\rho}})
;

3.

Fusion rule is given by the fusion rule of irreducible representations
a\times b=\sum_{c}N_{ab}^{c}c
.

Thus to understand these physical data, we need to explore the (unitary) representation category of
W
.

A unitary module
M
is a
W
-module with an inner product such that
\langle x,h\triangleright y\rangle=\langle h^{*}\triangleright x,y\rangle
, for all
h\in W
,
x,y\in M
(
h^{*}
is the
C^{*}
involution of
h
).
It is proved that
\mathsf{Rep}(W)
is a unitary multi-fusion category
Nill 1998
;
Nikshych et al. 2003
;
Böhm et al. 2011
.

The vacuum sector is given by the Gelfand–Naimark–Segal (GNS) representation associated with the counit
\varepsilon
.
More specifically, the module of vacuum sector is
\mathds{1}=W_{L}
, whose
W
-module structure is given by
h\triangleright x=\varepsilon_{L}(hx)
, for
h\in W
and
x\in W_{L}
. The inner product is chosen as
\langle x,y\rangle:=\varepsilon(x^{*}y)
.
Notice that there are several equivalent choices of vacuum modules, like
\hat{W}_{L},\hat{W}_{R}
, and
W_{R}
with their corresponding module structures
(
Böhm et al. 1999
, Lemma 2.12)
.
The vacuum sector is in general not simple, so it can be decomposed into the direct sum of some particle sectors
\mathds{1}=a_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus a_{n}
.
The fusion (tensor product) of two
W
-modules
M,\,N
are defined by
M\otimes N:=\{x\in M\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}N\,|\,x=\Delta(1)\,x\}

5
5

5

It is defined in this way to ensure that the identity
1
acts as the identity of the tensor product of modules.
, and its
W
-module structure is given by
h\triangleright(x\otimes y)=\sum_{(h)}(h^{(1)}\triangleright x)\otimes(h^{(2)}\triangleright y)
.
The tensor product of module morphisms
f_{1},f_{2}
is defined as the restriction of
f_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}f_{2}
to the subspace
M\otimes N
.

Since
\mathsf{Rep}(W)
is a rigid category, the antiparticle of
a=(\rho,M)
is given by the dual
\bar{a}=(\bar{\rho},\bar{M})
. Here
\bar{M}=\hat{M}=\mathrm{Hom}(M,\mathbb{C})
, where the left
W
-module structure is given by
x\triangleright\phi=\phi(S(x)\bullet)
.
Since
W
is a
C^{*}
weak Hopf algebra, the left dual and right dual of a
W
-module are isomorphic (
M^{\vee}\cong{{}^{\vee}}M=:\bar{M}
).
The inner product of
\bar{M}
is canonically induced by inner product of
M
,
\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle:=\langle x,y\rangle
.

Definition 7
.

For a fusion anyon model
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(W)
, a subset of simple anyons (irreps)
\mathcal{A}\subset\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Irr}(W)
is called fusion closed if it is (i) conjugation closed:
a\in\mathcal{A}\Rightarrow\bar{a}\in\mathcal{A}
; (ii) tensor product closed:
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, and
a\times b=\sum_{c}N_{ab}^{c}c
, then
c\in\mathcal{A}
for all
N_{ab}^{c}\neq 0
.

For a
(2+1)D
phase, besides the charge type, antiparticle, and fusion rule, there is some extra data called braiding that characterizes the mutual statistics of these charges.
The weak Hopf algebra in this case needs to possess a quasitriangular structure.

A quasitriangular weak Hopf algebra
(W,R)
is a weak Hopf algebra
W
equipped with an element
R=\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in\Delta^{\rm op}(1)(W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W)\Delta(1)
which satisfies

1.

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(R)=R_{13}R_{12}
,

2.

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(R)=R_{13}R_{23}
,

3.

\Delta^{\rm op}(h)R=R\Delta(h),\forall\,h\in W
,

4.

there exists
\tilde{R}\in\Delta(1)(W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W)\Delta^{\rm op}(1)
such that
\tilde{R}R=\Delta(1)
and
R\tilde{R}=\Delta^{\rm op}(1)
,

where we have used the notation
R_{kl}=\sum_{i}1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1\otimes a_{i}\otimes 1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1\otimes b_{i}\otimes 1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1
with
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
appearing in the
k
-th and
l
-th places respectively, whose length depends on the context.
For a weak Hopf algebra
W
, its quantum double
D(W)
has a quasitriangular structure
Nikshych et al. 2003
. We also include detailed proof of this fact in Appendix
A
for completeness.

3.2
Weak Hopf symmetry breaking

In this part, we generalize the results of Hopf symmetry breaking
Bais et al. 2003
to the weak Hopf symmetry case.
Consider a system with vacuum sector
\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}}
that has a weak Hopf symmetry
W
. After condensation, the vacuum of the condensed phase is the sea of vacuum charges. The
W
symmetry is broken and the vacuum sector becomes
\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}^{\prime}}
. The residual symmetry of the condensate is a weak Hopf subalgebra
V=\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}^{\prime}})\subset W
.
The excitation of the effective theory must carry an irreducible representation of
\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}^{\prime}})
.
Some charges of the original phase get confined, and others are free (deconfined).
These deconfined particles are fusion closed, and they are irreducible representations of a new weak Hopf symmetry
U
, which is the quotient of
V
. In this part we shall prove the following theorem:

Theorem 1
.

Consider a quantum phase
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(W)
with weak Hopf symmetry
W
, after the formation of condensate, the symmetry is broken into a weak Hopf subalgebra
V\hookrightarrow W
.
The new phase is given by
\mathcal{D}=\mathsf{Rep}(V)
. In this new phase, the particles that have nontrivial monodromy with condensate are confined, and the particles that have trivial monodromy with condensate are deconfined.
The set
\mathcal{A}_{\rm dc}
of deconfined particles are fusion closed.
These deconfined particles are irreducible representations of a new weak Hopf symmetry
U
, which is a weak Hopf quotient of
V
.

Symmetry breaking:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Rep}(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Rep}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Rep}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(3.1)

where
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are monoidal functors,
\iota
is an injective weak Hopf map (embedding), and
\pi
is a surjective weak Hopf map (quotient).

To understand the weak Hopf symmetry breaking, it is crucial to understand the structure of weak Hopf subalgebras and quotients. By definition,
a weak Hopf morphism
f:W\to V
is a weak bialgebra morphism such that
S_{V}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f=f\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S_{W}
.
The weak Hopf subalgebra and quotient are naturally defined as follows:

Definition 8
.

Let
W,V
be weak Hopf algebras. (i)
V
is called a weak Hopf quotient of
W
if there is a surjective weak Hopf morphism
f:W\twoheadrightarrow V
; (ii)
V
is called a weak Hopf subalgebra if there is an injective weak Hopf morphism
f:V\hookrightarrow W
.

It is clear that the image
\operatorname{Im}f
of a weak Hopf morphism
f
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of the target weak Hopf algebra.
As we have pointed out before, the weak Hopf algebra is self-dual, and the dual algebra of
W
is also a weak Hopf algebra.
The subalgebra and quotient of
W
and
\hat{W}
are also mutually related.

Lemma 1
.

If
f:W\to V
is a weak Hopf morphism, then the dual map
\hat{f}:\hat{V}\to\hat{W}
is also a weak Hopf morphism.
Moreover, if
f
is injective then
\hat{f}
is surjective; if
f
is surjective, then
\hat{f}
is injective.
This further implies that

1.

If
V
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of
W
, then
\hat{V}
is a weak Hopf quotient of
\hat{W}
.

2.

If
V
is a weak Hopf quotient of
W
, then
\hat{V}
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of
\hat{W}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\hat{f}
is a weak bialgebra morphism. For any
\varphi\in\hat{V}
and
x\in W
,

\langle(\hat{f}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\hat{S}_{V})(\varphi),x\rangle=\langle\varphi,(S_{V}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f)(x)\rangle=\langle\varphi,(f\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S_{W})(x)\rangle=\langle(\hat{S}_{W}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\hat{f})(\varphi),x\rangle.

(3.2)

This means
\hat{f}
is a weak Hopf morphism.
Since the dual of the injective map is surjective and the dual of the surjective map is injective, we complete the proofs of 1 and 2.
∎

The above result means that we can equivalently describe the weak Hopf symmetry breaking using weak Hopf subalgebra
f:V\hookrightarrow W
or using quotient
\hat{f}:\hat{W}\twoheadrightarrow\hat{V}
.
For a weak Hopf symmetry, the physical data, like particle type, antiparticle, and fusion rule, of the system with a weak Hopf quotient symmetry has a one-to-one correspondence with that of the original system.
This correspondence is characterized by the representation factors over the quotient map.
For a weak Hopf quotient map
f:W\to V
, a representation of
W
which factors over
f
is defined as
\rho=\sigma\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f
, where
\sigma
is a representation of
V
.

Lemma 2
.

Let
f:W\to V
be a quotient map of weak Hopf algebras, then the physical data of
V
-symmetry model are in one-to-one correspondence with the physical data of
W
-symmetry that factors over
f
.

1.

For a charge type
a=(\sigma,M_{\sigma})
of
V
-symmetry model, there is a corresponding charge type
a_{f}=(\rho=\sigma\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f,M_{\sigma})
.
If
\rho
is an irreducible representation of
V
, it is also an irreducible representation of
W
.

2.

The correspondence preserves conjugation of particles
\overline{a_{f}}=(\bar{\rho}=\bar{\sigma}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f,M_{\bar{\sigma}})=\bar{a}_{f}
.

3.

The correspondence preserves fusion rules.

4.

If
W
is semisimple, then
V
is also semisimple.

Proof.

1. If
\sigma:V\to\operatorname{End}(M_{\sigma})
is a representation of
V
, then
\rho=\sigma\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f
is a representation of
W
factors over
f
; and vice versa.
If
\sigma
is irreducible then there is no nontrivial submodule that is invariant under the action of
\sigma
. Since
f
is surjective, this also implies that there is no nontrivial submodule that is invariant under the action of
\rho
. If
\rho
is irreducible, a similar argument shows that
\sigma
is irreducible.

2. Since the matrix of conjugation of a representation

\zeta
satisfies
\bar{\zeta}(g)_{ij}=\zeta(S(g))_{ji}=\zeta_{ji}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S(g)
for all
g
, we see that
\bar{\sigma}_{ij}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f=\sigma_{ji}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S_{V}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f=\sigma_{ji}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S_{W}=\rho_{ji}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S_{W}=\bar{\rho}_{ij}
. Notice that we have used the fact that
f
is a weak Hopf morphism.

3. For fusion of particles, suppose that
\rho_{1}=\sigma_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f
and
\rho_{2}=\sigma_{2}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f
. By definition of tensor product of two representations, we have
(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{W}=(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(f\otimes f)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{W}=(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{V}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f
by the fact that
f
is a weak bialgebra morphism. Then using the fact that
f
is surjective, we see that the fusion rule remains unchanged under the correspondence.

4. It is a direct corollary of 1.
∎

Lemma 3
.

For a weak Hopf algebra
W
and a fusion closed set
\mathcal{A}\subset\operatorname{Irr}(W)
, the linear space
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
generated by the matrix elements of representations in
\mathcal{A}
is a weak Hopf semi-subalgebra of
\hat{W}
,
viz.
,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
is closed under multiplication, coclosed under comultiplication, and closed under antipode.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
is closed under multiplication
\hat{\mu}
of
\hat{W}
. This is clear from
\hat{\mu}(\rho_{ij}\sigma_{kl})=((\rho_{ij}\otimes\sigma_{kl})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{W})
. The product of matrix elements of two representations is the matrix elements of the tensor product of those two representations, and since
\mathcal{A}
is fusion closed, it is also in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

(iii)
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
is coclosed under coproduct of
\hat{W}
. From pairing
\langle\hat{\Delta}(\rho_{ij}),x\otimes y\rangle=\rho_{ij}(xy)=\sum_{k}\rho_{ik}(x)\rho_{kj}(y)
, we see that
\hat{\Delta}(\rho_{ij})=\sum_{k}\rho_{ik}\otimes\rho_{kj}
. Thus
\hat{\Delta}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}})\subset\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

(iv)
\hat{S}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}})\subset\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
. This is clear from
\hat{S}(\rho_{ij})=\rho_{ij}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S=\bar{\rho}_{ji}
.
∎

It is natural to introduce the weak Hopf subalgebra generated by fusion closed set
\mathcal{A}
as the minimal weak Hopf subalgebra of
\hat{W}
that contains
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We denote it as
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Since the double-dual
\hat{\hat{W}}
can be canonically identified with
W
, using the above-established results, we will see that each weak Hopf subalgebra
V\hookrightarrow W
is determined by a fusion closed set of anyons.
For a weak Hopf quotient algebra, a similar result holds.

Theorem 2
.

Consider a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
weak Hopf algebra
W
.

1.

Let
V\hookrightarrow W
be a weak Hopf subalgebra, then
V
is determined by a fusion closed subset
\mathcal{A}\subset\operatorname{Irr}(\hat{W})
. More precisely,
V
is
isomorphic to the weak Hopf subalgebra
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{A}}
generated by a fusion closed set
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

Let
W\twoheadrightarrow V
be a weak Hopf quotient, then
V
is determined by a fusion closed subset
\mathcal{A}\subset\operatorname{Irr}(W)
. More precisely,
V
is isomorphic to the dual weak Hopf algebra
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}
.

Proof.

1. From Lemma
1
we see that
\hat{V}
is a quotient of
\hat{W}
.
It is clear that
\mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Irr}(\hat{V})
of
\hat{V}
is a fusion closed set of
\hat{V}
. By Lemma
2
, it is also a fusion closed set of
W
. Since weak Hopf algebra
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{A}}
generated by
\mathcal{A}
is isomorphic to
V\simeq\hat{\hat{V}}
, we complete the proof.

2. From Lemma
1
,
\hat{V}
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of
\hat{W}
. Then using 1, we obtain the assertion.
∎

The above result provides an operational method to determine the subalgebra and/or quotient algebra of a given weak Hopf algebra. For a given weak Hopf symmetry, if we have the information of particles, then we can determine all the sub and quotient algebras.

Now let us turn back to the weak Hopf symmetry breaking.
The particles of a quantum phase with weak Hopf symmetry
W
are irreducible representations.
The formation of condensate particles in a new vacuum sector
\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}^{\prime}}
breaks weak Hopf symmetry
W
to weak Hopf stabilizer of the new vacuum sector. The residual weak Hopf symmetry is thus a weak Hopf subalgebra
W\hookleftarrow V=\operatorname{Stab}_{W}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}^{\prime}})
.
The particles in this new phase are irreducible representations of
V
.
In this new phase, not all particles are free, the particles that have nontrivial monodromy with condensate particles are confined.
The one with trivial monodromy with condensate particles is deconfined.
Two natural requirements of deconfined particles are that their antiparticles are also deconfined, and the tensor product of any two deconfined particles is deconfined.
This means that the set
\mathcal{A}_{\rm dc}\subset\operatorname{Irr}(V)
of deconfined particles in the new phase is fusion closed.
This
\mathcal{A}_{\rm dc}
will generate a weak Hopf subalgebra
\hat{U}\hookrightarrow\hat{V}
.
And
U\cong\hat{\hat{U}}\twoheadleftarrow V
is a quotient of
V
.
The deconfined particles are representations of
U
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

4
Two-dimensional lattice model

In this section, we present the lattice gauge theory whose symmetry is given by a finite-dimensional weak Hopf algebra
D(W)
.
This construction was proposed by one of the authors in
Chang 2014
. Here we will give a more detailed and comprehensive investigation, including the lattice model construction and ribbon operators.

4.1
Weak Hopf quantum double model

Consider a given 2
d
closed oriented manifold
\Sigma
, a lattice on it (a.k.a. a cellulation of
\Sigma
) is
C(\Sigma)=V(\Sigma)\cup E(\Sigma)\cup F(\Sigma)
, where
V(\Sigma)
,
E(\Sigma)
and
F(\Sigma)
are sets of vertices, edges, and faces, respectively.
The dual lattice of
C(\Sigma)
is a lattice
\tilde{C}(\Sigma)
for which
the vertices and faces of the original lattice are switched while the edge set remains unchanged.
A directed lattice is a lattice such that each edge
e\in E(\Sigma)
is assigned a direction. The direction of the corresponding dual edge
\tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}(\Sigma)
is defined by rotating the direction of
e
counterclockwise by
\pi/2
.
A site
s=(v,f)
is a pair of vertex
v
and an adjacent face
f
. Two sites
s,s^{\prime}
are called adjacent if they share a common vertex or face.

Let
W
be a fixed weak Hopf algebra. Then the total Hilbert space is
\mathcal{H}_{\rm tot}=\otimes_{e\in E(\Sigma)}W
. The
W
is a left
W
-module with respect to the following two actions
L_{\pm}
and also a left
\hat{W}
-module with respect to the actions
T_{\pm}
:

\displaystyle L_{+}^{h}|x\rangle=|h\triangleright x\rangle=|hx\rangle,

(4.1)

\displaystyle L_{-}^{h}|x\rangle=|x\triangleleft S^{-1}(h)\rangle=|xS^{-1}(h)\rangle,

(4.2)

\displaystyle T_{+}^{\varphi}|x\rangle=|\varphi\rightharpoonup x\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,x^{(2)}\rangle x^{(1)}\rangle,

(4.3)

\displaystyle T_{-}^{\varphi}|x\rangle=|x\leftharpoonup\hat{S}(\varphi)\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\hat{S}(\varphi),x^{(1)}\rangle x^{(2)}\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,S(x^{(1)})\rangle x^{(2)}\rangle,

(4.4)

where
h\in W
and
\varphi\in\hat{W}
. Here, we have adopted Sweedler’s arrow notations.
Similarly, we have the right module actions:

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{-}^{h}|x\rangle=|x\triangleleft h\rangle=|xh\rangle,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{+}^{h}|x\rangle=|S^{-1}(h)\triangleright x\rangle=|S^{-1}(h)x\rangle,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{T}_{-}^{\varphi}|x\rangle=|x\leftharpoonup\varphi\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,x^{(1)}\rangle x^{(2)}\rangle,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{T}_{+}^{\varphi}|x\rangle=|\hat{S}(\varphi)\rightharpoonup x\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\hat{S}(\varphi),x^{(2)}\rangle x^{(1)}\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,S(x^{(2)})\rangle x^{(1)}\rangle.

(4.8)

In this work, we will construct the lattice Hamiltonian based on the left module actions. Nevertheless, it will become clear later that the right actions are necessary for the construction of ribbon operators.

For a given directed edge
e
, we assign
L_{-}
and
L_{+}
to its starting and ending vertices respectively.
Similarly, for the dual edge
\tilde{e}
, we assign
T_{+}
and
T_{-}
to its starting and ending faces respectively.
Fix a site
s=(v,f)
, we order the edges around the vertex
v
and the dual edges around the face
f
counterclockwise with the origin
s
(the operators act on edges from the left-hand side of edges).
We then introduce two kinds of local operators associated with
s
. The first one is attached to the vertex of
s
: for
h\in W
,

A^{h}(s)\big|\begin{aligned} \hbox to56.7pt{\vbox to52.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.35054pt\lower-26.15262pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

(4.9)

If
h
is cocommutative, the corresponding operator will
not depend on
s
but only on
v
, and thus we will denote it as
A_{v}^{h}
.
The second one is attached to the face of
s
: for
\varphi\in\hat{W}
,

(4.10)

If
\varphi
is cocommutative, the corresponding operator will not depend on
s
but only on
f
. We will denote it as
B_{f}^{\varphi}
.

Proposition 1
.

For any site
s
, the operators
A^{h}(s)
and
B^{\varphi}(s)
satisfy the commutation relations

\displaystyle A^{h}(

\displaystyle s)B^{\varphi}(s)=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}B^{\varphi^{(2)}}(s)A^{h^{(2)}}(s)\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle,

(4.11)

\displaystyle B^{\varphi}(s)

\displaystyle A^{xh}(s)=B^{\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)}(s)A^{h}(s),\quad B^{\varphi}(s)A^{yh}(s)=B^{\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)}(s)A^{h}(s),

(4.12)

for any
x\in W_{L}
,
y\in W_{R}
. Therefore, the map

\rho:D(W)\to\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{H}(s)),\quad[\varphi\otimes h]\mapsto D^{\varphi\otimes h}(s)=B^{\varphi}(s)A^{h}(s)

(4.13)

is an algebra homomorphism, that is,
\rho
is a representation of
D(W)
over
\mathcal{H}(s)=\otimes_{j\in\partial s}W
.

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.88797pt}{12.82361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{
