{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.12569pt}{32.74045pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-10.79115pt}{12.82361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} &A^{h}(s)=\sum_{(h)}L_{-}^{h^{(1)}}(j_{1})\otimes L_{+}^{h^{(2)}}(j_{2})\otimes L_{+}^{h^{(3)}}(j_{3})\otimes L_{-}^{h^{(4)}}(j_{4}),\\
&B^{\varphi}(s)=\sum_{(\varphi)}T_{-}^{\varphi^{(1)}}(j_{4})\otimes T_{-}^{\varphi^{(2)}}(j_{5})\otimes T_{+}^{\varphi^{(3)}}(j_{6})\otimes T_{+}^{\varphi^{(4)}}(j_{1}).\end{aligned}

(4.14)

To prove the first identity, on the one hand, we see that

\displaystyle\quad A^{h}(s)B^{\varphi}(s)|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{6}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S(x_{4}^{(1)})S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}\right)\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)}),h^{(2)}x_{2},h^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(4)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}B^{\varphi^{(2)}}(s)A^{h^{(2)}}(s)\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{6}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S^{-1}(h^{(8)})h^{(7)}S(x_{4}^{(1)})S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)})h^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(3)}),h^{(4)}x_{2},h^{(5)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(6)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(6)}))S(x_{4}^{(1)})S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(1)}))\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|S^{-1}(S(x_{1}^{(1)}))S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{2},h^{(4)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S(x_{4}^{(1)})S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(1)}))),h^{(3)}x_{2},h^{(4)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)}\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(6)})),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S(x_{4}^{(1)})S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)}),h^{(2)}x_{2},h^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(4)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle.

Here in the third equality we used
\sum_{(w)}zS(w^{(1)})\otimes w^{(2)}=\sum_{(w)}S(w^{(1)})\otimes w^{(2)}z
for any
z\in W_{L}
and
w\in W
; the last equality follows since
w=\sum_{(w)}w^{(1)}\varepsilon_{R}(w^{(2)})
and
\sum_{(w)}w^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(w^{(1)}))=\sum_{(w)}S^{-1}(S(w^{(1)})w^{(2)}S(w^{(3)}))=S^{-1}(S(w))=w
. To prove the second identity, one proceeds

\displaystyle\quad B^{\varphi}(s)A^{xh}(s)|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{6}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(xh)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S(x_{4}^{(1)}S^{-1}((xh)^{(6)})S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}((xh)^{(1)})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}((xh)^{(2)}),(xh)^{(3)}x_{2},(xh)^{(4)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}((xh)^{(5)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}\varphi\left(S(x_{4}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(6)}))S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)})S^{-1}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{2},h^{(4)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{i})}(S^{-1}(x)\rightharpoonup\varphi)\left(S(x_{4}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(6)}))S(x_{5}^{(1)})x_{6}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\big|x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{2},h^{(4)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)}),x_{5}^{(2)},x_{6}^{(1)}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}B^{\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)}(s)\big|x_{1}S^{-1}(h^{(1)}),h^{(2)}x_{2},h^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}S^{-1}(h^{(4)}),x_{5},x_{6}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=B^{\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)}(s)A^{h}(s)|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{6}\rangle,

since
\Delta(xh)=\sum_{(h)}xh^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}
, and
S^{-1}(x)\rightharpoonup\varphi=\varphi(S^{-1}(x)\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)=\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)
. The third identity is proved similarly.
The last assertion follows from (
4.11
) and (
4.12
).
∎

Proposition 2
.

If
h
and
\varphi
are the respective Haar integrals of
W
and
\hat{W}
, then
A_{v}^{h}
and
B_{f}^{\varphi}
are Hermitian projectors, i.e.,
(A^{h}_{v})^{\dagger}=A^{h}_{v}
,
(A^{h}_{v})^{2}=(A^{h}_{v})
,

(B_{f}^{\varphi})^{\dagger}=B_{f}^{\varphi}
,
(B_{f}^{\varphi})^{2}=B_{f}^{\varphi}
, and
[A^{h}_{v},B_{f}^{\varphi}]=0
for all
v,f
.

Proof.

In
Chang 2014
, Chang proved that for cocommutative
h,\varphi
,
[A^{h}_{v},B_{f}^{\varphi}]=0
for all
v,f
.
We only need to show that they are Hermitian projectors.
Unlike
C^{*}
Hopf algebra case,
S
is now not equal to
S^{-1}
,
(L_{-}^{g})^{\dagger}\neq L^{g^{*}}_{-}
.
Recall that the inner product is given by
\langle x,y\rangle=\varphi_{\hat{W}}(x^{*}y)
;
we have

\langle x,g\triangleright y\rangle=\langle g\triangleright x,y\rangle,\quad\langle x,y\triangleleft S(g)\rangle=\langle x\triangleleft S^{-1}(g),y\rangle,

(4.15)

where we have used
S(x)^{*}=S^{-1}(x^{*})
.
Nevertheless, for Haar integral
S(h_{W})=S^{-1}(h_{W})=h_{W}
, we have
S(h_{W}^{(i)})=S^{-1}(h_{W}^{(i)})
, which further implies that

(A^{h_{W}}_{v})^{\dagger}=A^{h_{W}^{*}}_{v}=A^{h_{W}}_{v}
.
Since
h^{2}=h
,
(A^{h}_{v})^{2}=(A^{h}_{v})
.

The proof for the face operator is subtler. Using the fact that
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
is a left and a right integral, it satisfies (the dual version of
(
Böhm et al. 1999
, Eqs. (3.4a) and (3.4d))
)

\displaystyle(y\leftharpoonup\psi)\rightharpoonup\varphi_{\hat{W}}=\hat{S}(\psi)(y\rightharpoonup\varphi_{\hat{W}}),

(4.16)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\hat{W}}\leftharpoonup(\psi\rightharpoonup y)=(\varphi_{\hat{W}}\leftharpoonup y)\hat{S}(\psi).

(4.17)

When acting on
x^{*}
for both sides of Eq. (
4.17
), we obtain

\operatorname{LHS}=\sum\psi(y^{(2)})\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(1)}(y^{(1)})\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(2)}(x^{*})=\langle x,\psi\rightharpoonup y\rangle,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\operatorname{RHS}=\sum\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(1)}(y)\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(2)}((x^{*})^{(1)})\hat{S}(\psi)((x^{*})^{(2)})=\sum\varphi_{\hat{W}}((x^{*})^{(1)}y)\langle\psi,S((x^{*})^{(2)})\rangle.

(4.19)

For Haar integral
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
,
\hat{S}(\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)})=\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)})
.
Take
\psi
in Eqs. (
4.18
) and (
4.19
) as
\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)}
, we obtain

\langle x,\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)}\rightharpoonup y\rangle=\langle(\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)})^{*}\rightharpoonup x,y\rangle.

(4.20)

Thus
(T_{+}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)}})^{\dagger}=T_{+}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{*(i)}}=T_{+}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)}}
.
Similarly, we have
(T_{-}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)}})^{\dagger}=T_{-}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{*(i)}}=T_{-}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{(i)}}
.
Therefore,
(B^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}_{f})^{\dagger}=B^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}_{f}
. Since
\varphi_{\hat{W}}^{2}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, we see that
B_{f}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}
is a projector.
∎

Denote
A_{v}=A_{v}^{h_{W}}
and
B_{f}=B_{f}^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}
, where
h_{W}
and
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
are Haar integrals of
W
and
\hat{W}
respectively. The local commutating projector Hamiltonian is given by

H[D(W);C(\Sigma)]=\sum_{v\in V(\Sigma)}(I-A_{v})+\sum_{f\in F(\Sigma)}(I-B_{f}),

(4.21)

where
A_{v}
imposes a local Gauss constraint and
B_{f}
imposes a local flatness condition.
Since we have
{L}_{-}^{h}=S\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}L_{+}^{h}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{-1}
,
T_{-}^{\phi}=S^{-1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}T_{+}^{\phi}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S
, if the direction of
e
is reversed, we just act the antipode on it. This means that the initial configuration of the lattice edge direction does not matter.
We will denote the quantum double model obtained in this way as
\mathsf{QD}(\hat{W},W;\Sigma)
.
As we will see, its topological excitation is given by the representation category
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
, which is a braided fusion category.

Remark 4.1
.

If we use the right-module structure, and order edges around the vertex and face clockwise, we will similarly obtain vertex and face operators
\tilde{A}^{h}(s)
and
\tilde{B}^{\varphi}(s)
.

Remark 4.2
.

Since
W
is finite dimensional, for each
\varphi\in\hat{W}
, there is a corresponding
g_{\varphi}\in W
such that
\varphi(x)=\langle g_{\varphi},x\rangle
for all
x\in W
.

Remark 4.3
.

For any given weak Hopf subalgebras
J\subset\hat{W}
and
K\subset W
for which there is a pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:J\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
induced by the canonical pairing of
\hat{W}
and
W
, we can construct a lattice gauge theory model such that the topological excitation is given by the representation category of the generalized quantum double
J^{\rm cop}\Join K
(the Hopf algebra case has be briefly discussed in
Jia et al. 2022
).

Weak Hopf symmetry of the model.
—  The vacuum sector of the weak Hopf quantum double model is given by

\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}}=\prod_{v}(I-A_{v})\prod_{f}(I-B_{f})\mathcal{H}_{\rm tot}.

(4.22)

For any ground state
|\Omega\rangle\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}}
, we have

\displaystyle B^{\psi}A^{g}|\Omega\rangle

\displaystyle=B^{\psi}A^{g}A^{h_{W}}B^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}|\Omega\rangle=B^{\psi}A^{\varepsilon_{L}(g)h_{W}}B^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}|\Omega\rangle

\displaystyle=B^{\psi(\varepsilon_{L}(g)\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\varphi_{\hat{W}}}A^{h_{W}}|\Omega\rangle=B^{(S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(g))\rightharpoonup\psi)\varphi_{\hat{W}}}A^{h_{W}}|\Omega\rangle

\displaystyle=B^{\hat{\varepsilon}_{L}((S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(g))\rightharpoonup\psi))\varphi_{\hat{W}}}A^{h_{W}}|\Omega\rangle=B^{\hat{\varepsilon}_{L}((S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(g))\rightharpoonup\psi))}A^{1_{W}}|\Omega\rangle.

Generalized weak Hopf quantum double model and hierarchy structure.
—  Consider two weak Hopf algebras
J,W
equipped with a pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:J\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
, we could construct a generalized quantum double
D(J^{\rm cop},W)
.
Using a very similar lattice construction, we could obtain a generalized weak Hopf quantum double model
\mathsf{QD}(J,W;\Sigma)
on a lattice
\Sigma
.
The topological excitation is given by the representation category of the generalized quantum double
\mathsf{Rep}(D(J^{\rm cop},W))
.
It is interesting here that we have a hierarchy structure, namely, we could choose weak Hopf subalgebras
J^{\prime}\subset J
and
W^{\prime}\subset W
to build a quantum double model
\mathsf{QD}(J^{\prime},W^{\prime};\Sigma)
.
This theory is a sub-theory of the original theory in the sense that all topological excitation can be obtained from the original theory by restriction of the original weak Hopf algebras to their subalgebras.

Topological excitations.
—
Let us now provide an outline of the classification of the topological excitations for the weak Hopf quantum double model.
A more rigorous and comprehensive treatment will be given in our forthcoming work
Jia and Tan tiona
.

Before delving into the exploration of topological excitations within the weak Hopf algebraic quantum double model, it is pertinent to revisit the case where the algebraic structure is given by
W=\mathbb{C}[G]
, with
G
representing a finite group
Kitaev 2003
;
Lusztig 1987
;
Dijkgraaf et al. 1991
;
Gould 1993
;
Witherspoon 1996
.
In this particular case, topological excitations can be classified using the notation
([g],\pi)
, where
[g]
represents a conjugacy class of the group
G
, and
\pi
denotes an irreducible representation of the centralizer
C_{G}(g)
. It is worth noting that there exists a
\mathbb{C}C_{G}(g)
-module, denoted as
M_{\pi}
, corresponding to the representation
\pi
. Consequently, a topological charge can be expressed as follows:

a_{[g],\pi}=\mathbb{C}[G]\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}C_{G}(g)}}M_{\pi}.

(4.23)

Furthermore, it is essential to mention that the vacuum charge corresponds to the case where
g=e_{G}
(the identity element of the group
G
) and
\pi=\mathds{1}
, indicating the trivial representation.
The antiparticle of the topological excitation described in Eq. (
4.23
) is given by (notably,
C_{G}(g)=C_{G}(g^{-1})
):

a_{[g^{-1}],\pi^{\dagger}}=\mathbb{C}[G]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}C_{G}(g^{-1})}M_{\pi^{\dagger}}.

(4.24)

The conjugacy class
[g]
associated with a topological charge is referred to as the magnetic charge, while the irreducible representation
\pi
is known as the electric charge. In the case where
g=e_{G}
,
a_{[e_{G}],\pi}
is characterized by a representation of
G
and is referred to as a chargeon. When
\pi=\mathds{1}
(the trivial representation),
a_{[g],\mathds{1}}
is called a fluxion. Lastly, if both
g\neq e_{G}
and
\pi\neq\mathds{1}
,
a_{[g],\pi}
is referred to as a dyon.
The quantum dimension of a topological excitation is given by:

\operatorname{FPdim}a_{[g],\pi}=|[g]|\dim\pi,

(4.25)

where
|[g]|
represents the size of the conjugacy class and
\dim\pi
denotes the dimension of the irreducible representation. These topological excitations form a UMTC denoted as
\mathsf{Rep}(D(G))
, which represents the representation category of the quantum double of the finite group
G
.

In our previous work
Jia et al. 2022
, we extensively discussed the topological excitations within the context of the Hopf quantum double model based on Refs.
Gelaki and Nikshych 2008
;
Burciu 2012
. Notably, the insights and analysis presented in that study can be readily extended and applied to the weak Hopf quantum double model.
The topological excitations are classified by the irreducible representations of the quantum double
D(W)
of the weak Hopf algebra
W
, and these topological excitations form a braided fusion category
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
.
To make it clearer, we need to introduce the notion of
universal grading group
for a multi-fusion category
Gelaki and Nikshych 2008
.
A multi-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
is referred to as
G
-graded if it can be decomposed as follows:

\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{g\in G}\mathcal{C}_{g},

(4.26)

where
\mathcal{C}_{g}
represents full Abelian subcategories and the tensor product of objects from
\mathcal{C}_{g}
and
\mathcal{C}_{h}
maps to objects in
\mathcal{C}_{gh}
for all
g
and
h
in the finite group
G
. Here,
G
is known as the grading group of
\mathcal{C}
. When
G
is maximal in the sense that any other grading can be obtained by taking a quotient group of
G
, it is referred to as the universal grading group, denoted as
G=U(\mathcal{C})
.
The Grothendieck ring
K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
is a ring with a multiplicative structure induced by the tensor products of
\mathcal{C}
. Notice that

K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
is a multi-fusion ring when
\mathcal{C}
is a multi-fusion category.

For the weak Hopf quantum double model, we know that the topological excitations are classified by the irreducible representations of the quantum double
D(W)
.
Since
\mathsf{Rep}(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})
is a multi-fusion category, we have a corresponding universal grading group
G=U(\mathsf{Rep}(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}))
.
Then
\mathsf{Rep}(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})=\oplus_{g\in G}\mathsf{Rep}(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})_{g}
.
Suppose that
W_{g}
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of
W
such that
\mathsf{Rep}(\hat{W}_{g})=\oplus_{x\in C_{G}(g)}\mathsf{Rep}(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})x
. We denote by
\mathcal{I}_{g}={M_{g}}
the set of all irreducible representations of
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\Join W_{g}
(where “
\Join
” denotes the bicrossed product) satisfying the condition that the character
\chi_{M_{g}}
, when restricted to the largest central weak Hopf subalgebra
Z(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})
of
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}
, satisfies
{\chi_{M_{g}}}_{|{Z(\hat{W}^{\rm cop})}}=g\dim M_{g}
.
The topological excitations can be classified as follows:

a_{g,M_{g}}=W\otimes_{W_{g}}M_{g},

(4.27)

where
g\in G
and
M_{g}\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
. This classification provides a complete description of the irreducible representations of the quantum double
D(W)

Jia and Tan tiona
.
The element
g\in G
can be interpreted as a magnetic charge, while
M_{g}
represents an electric charge. Specifically, when
g=e_{G}
, the topological excitation
a_{e_{G},M_{e_{G}}}
is referred to as a chargeon. If
M_{g}=\mathds{1}
, the topological excitation
a_{g,\mathds{1}}
is called a fluxion. For the case where both
g\neq e_{G}
and
M_{g}\neq\mathds{1}
, the topological excitation
a_{g,M_{g}}
is known as a dyon.
The quantum dimension of a topological excitation is given by:

\operatorname{FPdim}a_{g,M_{g}}=\frac{|G|}{\dim W_{g}}\dim M_{g},

(4.28)

where
|G|
represents the order of the group
G
, and
\dim W_{g}
corresponds to the dimension of the subspace
W_{g}
.

4.2
Local algebra and ribbon operator

For a site
s
, the vertex and face operators will generate a local algebra

\mathcal{A}(s):=\{D^{\varphi\otimes h}(s)=B^{\varphi}(s)A^{h}(s),\varphi\in\hat{W},h\in W\}.

(4.29)

It is clear that
\mathcal{A}(s)\cong D(W)
is a weak Hopf algebra. In this subsection, we will construct the ribbon operators on the quantum double model on closed surfaces based on
W
. These operators form an algebra isomorphic to the dual of
D(W)
. The ribbon operators on the model based on Hopf algebras have been studied in Refs.
Yan et al. 2022
;
Jia et al. 2022
.

We begin with the basic ingredients of ribbons. The building blocks of a ribbon are direct/dual triangles. A direct triangle is a triangle on the original lattice, consisting of a directed edge and two adjacent sites such that the edge connects the two sites, and a dual triangle is a direct triangle on the dual lattice. A direct (resp. dual) triangle is denoted as
\tau=(e,s_{0},s_{1})
(resp.
\tilde{\tau}=(\tilde{e},s_{0},s_{1})
), where the ordered pair
(s_{0},s_{1})
indicates the direction of the triangle. A (direct or dual) triangle is called a left-handed (resp. right-handed) triangle if the edge of the triangle is on the left-hand (resp. right-hand) side when one passes through the triangle along its positive direction. We call this feature the chirality of the triangle (this is called local orientation in Ref.
Yan et al. 2022
). Left-handed (resp. right-handed) triangles are denoted as
\tau_{L}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{L}
(resp.
\tau_{R}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{R}
). We use a superscript
+
or
-
to indicate that the direction of
\tau=(e,s_{0},s_{1})
and
e
coincide or not (the same for
\tilde{\tau}
):

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.93755pt}{-46.00053pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}

(4.30)

A ribbon, denoted as
\rho
, consists of a sequence of consecutive triangles such that the ending site of a triangle is the starting site of the next triangle and that there is no self-overlapping. A ribbon is called direct (resp. dual) if it consists of only direct (resp. dual) triangles, otherwise it is called proper. A closed ribbon is one with the starting and ending sites being the same. There are two types of ribbons: type-A ribbons, denoted as
\rho_{A}
, are the ones consisting of left-handed direct triangles and right-handed dual triangles, while type-B ribbons, denoted as
\rho_{B}
, are the ones consisting of right-handed direct triangles and left-handed dual triangles. See Fig.
1
for an illustration. In the course of constructing the ribbon operators, we need to deal with different cases separately.

Figure 1:
Ribbon types. A type-A ribbon
\rho_{A}
(red) consists of left-handed direct triangles and right-handed dual triangles; a type-B ribbon
\rho_{B}
(blue) consists of right-handed direct triangles and left-handed dual triangles.

First we define the triangle operators. We use the same convention as in Ref.
Jia et al. 2022
(note that this is slightly different from the one in Ref.
Yan et al. 2022
). Specifically, the triangle operators are defined as follows:

\displaystyle F^{h,\varphi}(\tau_{R}^{+})|x\rangle=\varepsilon(h)T_{-}^{\varphi}|x\rangle,\quad F^{h,\varphi}(\tau_{R}^{-})|x\rangle=\varepsilon(h)T_{+}^{\varphi}|x\rangle,

(4.31)

\displaystyle F^{h,\varphi}(\tau_{L}^{-})|x\rangle=\varepsilon(h)\tilde{T}_{-}^{\varphi}|x\rangle,\quad F^{h,\varphi}(\tau_{L}^{+})|x\rangle=\varepsilon(h)\tilde{T}_{+}^{\varphi}|x\rangle,

(4.32)

\displaystyle F^{h,\varphi}(\tilde{\tau}_{R}^{+})|x\rangle=\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)L^{h}_{-}|x\rangle,\quad F^{h,\varphi}(\tilde{\tau}_{R}^{-})|x\rangle=\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)L^{h}_{+}|x\rangle,

(4.33)

\displaystyle F^{h,\varphi}(\tilde{\tau}_{L}^{-})|x\rangle=\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\tilde{L}_{-}^{h}|x\rangle,\quad F^{h,\varphi}(\tilde{\tau}_{L}^{+})|x\rangle=\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\tilde{L}_{+}|x\rangle,

(4.34)

for
x,\,h\in W
and
\varphi\in\hat{W}
. We will see later that, by the above choice of convention, the vertex and face operators are special cases of ribbon operators.

Next we define the ribbon operators for a general ribbon. The strategy is the same as that in the Hopf algebra case
Yan et al. 2022
. The ribbon operators for type-B ribbons
\rho=\rho_{B}
are parameterized by
h\otimes\varphi\in W^{\rm op}\otimes\hat{W}\simeq(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)^{\vee}
, denoted as
F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)
or
F^{h,\varphi}_{\rho}
. It only acts non-trivially on edges contained in
\rho
. Since
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
is an algebra, its dual has a coalgebra structure with comultiplication given by

\Delta(h\otimes\varphi)=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}(h^{(1)}\otimes\hat{k})\otimes(S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}\otimes\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)),

(4.35)

where
\{k\}
and
\{\hat{k}\}
are dual bases of
W
and
\hat{W}
. To define
F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)
, take a decomposition
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
such that both
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
have the same direction with
\rho
and the terminal site of
\rho_{1}
is the initial site of
\rho_{2}
. Then the ribbon operator
F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)
is defined recursively by

F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2}).

(4.36)

It is independent of the choice of the decomposition
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
by the co-associativity of
(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)^{\vee}
. The ribbon operators for type-A ribbons are parameterized by
h\otimes\varphi\in W\otimes\hat{W}^{\rm op}\simeq(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)^{\vee,\rm op}
, and the construction is similar by the recursive formula (
4.36
).

Let
s=(v,f)
be a site. There is a unique closed ribbon
\sigma_{v}
of type-A surrounding
v
which contains only dual triangles with one vertex
v
. Likewise, there is a unique closed ribbon
\sigma_{f}
of type-B inside
f
which contains only direct triangles with one vertex
\tilde{f}
, the vertex in the dual lattice corresponding to
f
. The directions of these closed ribbons are taken counterclockwise starting from the site
s
. Then one can see that
A^{h}(s)=F^{h,\varepsilon}(\sigma_{v})
and
B^{\varphi}(s)=F^{1_{W},\varphi}
for
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
and
\varphi\in\operatorname{Cocom}(\hat{W})
. Thus, the vertex and face operators are special cases of ribbon operators, as expected.

Note that
D(W)^{\vee}
can be identified with a subspace of
W^{\rm op}\otimes\hat{W}\simeq(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)^{\vee}
consisting of elements vanishing on the ideal
J
. With this identification, we have the following relations when the ribbon operators are parameterized by elements in
D(W)^{\vee}
.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose
\rho
is a ribbon with initial site
s
. If
g\otimes\psi\in D(W)^{\vee}
, and
x\in W_{L}
, then

\displaystyle F^{xg,\psi}(\rho)A^{h}(s)=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)A^{S(x)h}(s),

(4.37)

\displaystyle F^{xg,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi}(s)=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi\leftharpoonup x}(s).

(4.38)

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.
∎

Lemma 5
.

Let
\rho=\rho_{A}
or
\rho_{B}
be an open ribbon with starting and ending sites
s_{0},s_{1}
. Then the ribbon operator
F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)
satisfies the following commutation relations with local operators:

(1)

At the starting site
s_{0}
:

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{S^{-2}(g^{(1)})hS^{-1}(g^{(3)}),\varphi(S^{-1}(g^{(2)})\bullet)}(\rho_{A})A^{g^{(4)}}(s_{0}),

(4.39)

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{S^{-2}(g^{(2)})hS^{-1}(g^{(4)}),\varphi(S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\bullet)}(\rho_{B})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{0}),

(4.40)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\varphi}(\rho_{A})B^{\psi(\bullet S^{-1}(h^{(1)}))}(s_{0}),

(4.41)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\varphi}(\rho_{B})B^{\psi(S(h^{(1)})\bullet)}(s_{0}).

(4.42)

(2)

At the ending
s_{1}
:

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet S^{-2}(g^{(2)}))}(\rho_{A})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{1}),

(4.43)

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet g^{(1)})}(\rho_{B})A^{g^{(2)}}(s_{1}),

(4.44)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}\varphi(k^{(2)})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{A})B^{\psi(S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{1}),

(4.45)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}\varphi(k^{(2)})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{B})B^{\psi(\bullet S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)})}(s_{1}).

(4.46)

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.
∎

Now we show that the ribbon operators defined above are commuting with all stabilizer operators in the Hamiltonian except possibly those ones at the two ending sites of the underlying ribbons.

Proposition 3
.

Let
\rho
be a ribbon. If
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
and
\varphi\in\operatorname{Cocom}(\hat{W})
, then
A^{h}(s)
and
B^{\varphi}(s)
are commuting with
F^{g,\psi}(\rho)
for all
s\neq\partial_{0}\rho,\partial_{1}\rho
and
g\otimes\psi\in D(W)^{\vee}
:

A^{h}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho)=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)A^{h}(s),\quad B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho)=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi}(s).

(4.47)

Proof.

We prove the assertions for type-B ribbons. The proof for type-A ribbons is similar. Take the decomposition
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
such that
\partial_{1}\rho_{1}=\partial_{0}\rho_{2}=s
. Then by Eqs. (
4.40
) and (
4.44
), we have

\displaystyle\quad A^{h}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}A^{h}(s)F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}(\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{1})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}(\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(h^{(3)})S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)}),\psi(k^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(h^{(5)})S^{-1}(j^{(3)}h^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)}h^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(7)}),\psi(j^{(2)}h^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(6)})\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(4)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(h^{(5)})S^{-1}(j^{(3)}h^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})),\psi(j^{(2)}h^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(6)})\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(4)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(h^{(5)})S^{-1}(j^{(3)}h^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})h^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(6)})\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(4)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(h^{(4)})S^{-1}(j^{(3)}h^{(2)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)}))\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(3)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(h^{(4)})S^{-1}(j^{(3)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})h^{(2)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(3)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-2}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)}))S^{-1}(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)}))h^{(1)}}(s)

\displaystyle=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)A^{h}(s).

(4.48)

Here, in the fifth to ninth equalities, we used a cyclic rotation
h^{(i)}
to
h^{(i+1)}
since
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
, and the identity
\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}y\otimes x^{(2)}=\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}S(y)
for
y\in W_{R}
; the tenth equality follows from Eq. (
4.37
).

Similarly, by Eqs. (
4.42
) and (
4.46
), we have

\displaystyle\quad B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}\hat{k}(j^{(2)})F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})B^{\varphi(\bullet S^{-1}(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)})}(s)F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(3)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}\hat{k}(j^{(2)})F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(4)})g^{(4)}k^{(2)},\psi(k^{(3)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(k^{(1)})S(g^{(3)})k^{(5)}\bullet S^{-1}(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)})}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(j^{(5)})g^{(4)}j^{(3)},\psi(j^{(4)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(j^{(2)})S(g^{(3)})j^{(6)}\bullet S^{-1}(j^{(7)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)})}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(j^{(4)})g^{(4)}j^{(2)},\psi(j^{(3)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S^{-1}(j^{(6)})g^{(2)}\varepsilon_{L}(j^{(1)})S(g^{(3)})j^{(5)}\bullet)}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(j^{(4)})g^{(3)}\varepsilon_{L}(j^{(1)})j^{(2)},\psi(j^{(3)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S^{-1}(j^{(6)})\varepsilon_{L}(g^{(2)})j^{(5)}\bullet)}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(S(\varepsilon_{L}(g^{(2)}))j^{(3)})g^{(3)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi\leftharpoonup S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(j^{(4)}))}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(j^{(4)}))S^{-1}(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi}(s)

\displaystyle=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi}(s).

(4.49)

Here, the fifth equality follows from the fact that
\varphi\in\operatorname{Cocom}(\hat{W})
, the seventh from
\sum_{(y)}y^{(1)}\otimes xy^{(2)}=\sum_{(y)}S(x)y^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}
for
x\in W_{L}
, and the eighth from Eq. (
4.38
).
∎

Ribbon operator and topological excitation.
—
Let us now consider how to use the ribbon operators to create topological excitations.
We take the type-B ribbon as an example to illustrate the mechanism, and the type-A ribbon can be handled similarly.
Given a ribbon
\rho
, and ground state
|\Omega\rangle
, we define

\mathcal{V}_{\rho}=\{|\Omega^{h,\varphi}_{\rho}\rangle=F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)|\Omega\rangle\,|\,h\otimes\varphi\in D(W)^{\vee}\},

(4.50)

where we assume the ground state degeneracy is one for simplicity.
For the general case, we can gather all the
|\Omega^{h,\varphi}_{\rho}\rangle
corresponding to the ground states
|\Omega\rangle
together and form such a set.
Denote the two ends of the ribbon as
s_{i}=\partial_{i}\rho
(
i=0,1
). Then both the local operators
B^{\varphi}(s_{0})A^{h}(s_{0})
and
B^{\varphi}(s_{1})A^{h}(s_{1})
define representations of
D(W)
on
\mathcal{V}_{\rho}
.
This follows from Lemma
5
and
A^{h_{W}}|\Omega\rangle=|\Omega\rangle=B^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}|\Omega\rangle
.
This means that at two ends of the ribbon, there are two topological excitations created.

More precisely, by Proposition
1
, we see that the total space
\mathcal{V}=\otimes_{e\in E(\Sigma)}W
is a
D(W)
-module, and at each site
s
, there is a canonical representation of
D(W)
,
[\varphi\otimes h]\mapsto D^{\varphi\otimes h}(s)=B^{\varphi}(s)A^{h}(s)
.
A topological excitation is mathematically characterized by a representation
X
of
D(W)
(
viz.
,
X
is a
D(W)
-module).
If at a site
s
there is a topological excitation
X
, we denote the corresponding
D(W)
-module as
\mathcal{V}(s,X)
, which is a submodule of
\mathcal{V}
.
By the Artin-Wedderburn theorem, it is clear that

\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{X}N_{X}\mathcal{V}(s,X),

(4.51)

for a fixed site
s
, where
N_{X}
is a multiplicity of
\mathcal{V}(s,X)
(which is an integer). For
n
disjoint sites
s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}
, we define

\mathcal{V}(s_{1},X_{1};\cdots;s_{n},X_{n})=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}(s_{i},X_{i}).

(4.52)

The vacuum sector of the weak Hopf quantum double model is

\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}}:=\{\,|\Omega\rangle\,|\,A^{h}(s)|\Omega\rangle=\varepsilon(h)|\Omega\rangle,B^{\phi}(s)|\Omega\rangle=\hat{\varepsilon}(\phi)|\Omega\rangle,\forall\,h,\phi,\forall\,s\}.

(4.53)

Using the notation of Eq. (
4.52
), the vacuum sector is

\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}}=\bigcap_{s\,:\,\text{site}}\mathcal{V}(s,\mathds{1}),

(4.54)

viz.
, for all sites, the excitations are vacuum particle
\mathds{1}
.
The case of particular interest for our discussion of ribbon operators is when there are two excitations

\mathcal{V}(s_{0},X_{0};s_{1},X_{1}),

(4.55)

where
s_{0}=\partial_{0}\rho
and
s_{1}=\partial_{1}\rho
are two ends of a ribbon
\rho
.
For type-B ribbons, we have shown that the ribbon operator algebra
\mathcal{F}\cong D(W)^{\vee}
.
By the Artin-Wedderburn theorem for the quantum double
D(W)
and treating
D(W)^{\vee}
as a
D(W)|D(W)
-bimodule (the left and right actions are given by “
\rightharpoonup
” and “
\leftharpoonup
” respectively, see Eq. (
2.31
) for the definition), we have the decomposition
6
6

6

This is also known as the Peter-Weyl theorem in the context of Lie group theory.

D(W)^{\vee}=\bigoplus_{X\in\operatorname{Irr}(D(W))}X\otimes X^{\vee},

(4.56)

where
\operatorname{Irr}(D(W))
is the set of all equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
D(W)
.
Therefore
X\otimes X^{\vee}
can be embedded into
D(W)^{\vee}
. We denote the corresponding image in
D(W)^{\vee}
as
\operatorname{Proj}_{X}D(W)^{\vee}
; it is clear that
D(W)^{\vee}=\oplus_{X\in\operatorname{Irr}(D(W))}\operatorname{Proj}_{X}D(W)^{\vee}
.
The ribbon operator that creates topological excitations
X
and
X^{\vee}
at two ends of the ribbon
\rho
can be constructed by choosing elements
g\otimes\psi\in\operatorname{Proj}_{X}D(W)^{\vee}
, i.e.,

F^{g,\psi}_{\rho},g\otimes\psi\in\operatorname{Proj}_{X}D(W)^{\vee}.

(4.57)

When acting on the spaces with two given excitations
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, this kind of ribbon operators will induce the fusion of anyons at two ends
\mathcal{V}(s_{1},X_{0}\otimes X;s_{1},X_{1}\otimes X^{\vee})
.

5
Algebraic theory of gapped boundaries and domain walls

The gapped boundary of the topological phases is a crucial topic in understanding the topological phase.
The holographic boundary-bulk duality
Kong et al. 2017
asserts that all the bulk information can be recovered from the boundary by taking the center of the boundary phase.
In our previous work
Jia et al. 2022
, we establish the algebraic theory of gapped boundary and domain wall for the general Hopf quantum double model which can recover many existing results for the finite group boundary
Bravyi and Kitaev 1998
;
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
;
Beigi et al. 2011
;
Cong et al. 2017a
.
Here we will generalize the results there to the weak Hopf quantum double model.

5.1
Gapped boundary

For
2d
topological phase with input unitary (multi-) fusion category
\mathcal{C}
, the gapped boundary theory is determined by finite indecomposable module categories
\mathcal{M}
over
\mathcal{C}

Kitaev and Kong 2012
;
Fuchs et al. 2013
.
The boundary topological excitations are given by the category of all
\mathcal{C}
-module functors from
\mathcal{M}
to itself,
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})
.
For a bulk weak Hopf phase
D(W)
, we need to find an algebraic structure that fits into this formalism.
Our main observation is that the gapped boundary is characterized by a
W
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
or equivalently a
W
-module algebra
\mathfrak{M}
.
The mathematical discussion of these structures can be found in
Nill et al. 1998
;
Böhm 2000
;
Nikshych 2000
;
Henker 2011
.

Definition 9
.

Let
W
be a weak Hopf algebra:

(1) An algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is called a right
W
-comodule algebra if there is a comodule map
\beta:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}\otimes W
such that
\beta(xy)=\beta(x)\beta(y)
and
\beta(1_{\mathfrak{A}})(x\otimes 1_{W})=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{A}}\otimes\varepsilon_{L})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\beta(x)
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{A}
. We will adopt the Sweedler’s notation
\beta(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{[0]}\otimes x^{[1]}
.
7
7

7

We use square brackets to denote the comodule structure to distinguish it from the comultiplication of weak Hopf algebra.
The left
W
-comodule algebra can be defined similarly.

(2) An algebra
\mathfrak{M}
is called a left
W
-module algebra if
\mathfrak{M}
is a left
W
-module such that
h\triangleright(xy)=\sum_{(h)}(h^{(1)}\triangleright x)(h^{(2)}\triangleright y)
and
h\triangleright 1_{\mathfrak{M}}=\varepsilon_{L}(h)\triangleright 1_{\mathfrak{M}}
for all
h\in W
and
x\in\mathfrak{M}
. The right
W
-module algebra can be defined similarly.

Weak Hopf quantum double model

String-net model

Bulk

Hopf algebra
W

\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(W)

Bulk phase

\mathcal{D}=\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}|\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})

Boundary

W
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}

\mathcal{C}
-module category
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathcal{M}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}

Boundary phase

\mathcal{B}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}({{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathcal{M},{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathcal{M})

Boundary defect

{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}({{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathcal{M},{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathcal{M})

Table 1:
The dictionary between
W
-comodule algebra description of weak Hopf quantum double boundary and string-net boundary.

Let us first consider how to describe the gapped boundary via
W
-comodule algebra.
In the following, we assume
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(W)
.
For the gapped boundary determined by a
W
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, its corresponding
\mathcal{C}
-module is given by the category
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}
of finite dimensional
\mathfrak{A}
-modules.
It is proved in
(
Henker 2011
, Lemma 10.1.1)
that
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}
is a
\mathcal{C}
-module category.
First notice that
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
is finite semisimple, where the simple objects are just simple
\mathfrak{A}
-modules. Since
\dim\mathfrak{A}<\infty
, there are finite simple objects up to equivalence, thus
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}
is finite.
To make
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}
a
\mathcal{C}
-module, we need to introduce a bifunctor
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}\to{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}
.
To this end, notice that there is a left
\mathfrak{A}
-module structure over
X\otimes M
with
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
M\in{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
. More explicitly, the structure map
\mu_{X\otimes M}:\mathfrak{A}\otimes(X\otimes M)\to X\otimes M
is given by (in diagrammatic representation)

(5.1)

viz.
,
\mu_{X\otimes M}=(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{M})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes\tau_{\mathfrak{A},X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\beta_{\mathfrak{A}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})
, where
\mu_{X}
and
\mu_{M}
are the
W
-module structure and
\mathfrak{A}
-module structure maps of
X
and
M
respectively,
\beta_{\mathfrak{A}}
is the
W
-comodule structure map of
\mathfrak{A}
, and
\tau_{\mathfrak{A},X}
is the swap map.
The tensor product is defined as the submodule

X\otimes_{\mathfrak{A}}M:=\{x\,|\,\beta(1)\,x=x\}=\sum_{(1)}1^{[1]}X\otimes 1^{[0]}M.

(5.2)

The associator and identity morphisms can be defined naturally.

If
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are Morita equivalent, then
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
, hence they give the same gapped boundary.
Therefore, the gapped boundaries are classified by Morita equivalent classes of
W
-comodule algebras.
The boundary excitation can be regarded as a point defect between two boundaries of the same type.
Thus we can consider the more general defects between two gapped boundaries
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
and
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
, where the defects are classified by
\mathcal{C}
-module functors between
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
and
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
.
An Eilenberg-Watts type theorem
Eilenberg 1960
;
Watts 1960
shows that these boundary defects can equivalently be classified by the
\mathfrak{B}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules.

Theorem 3
.

The gapped boundaries of a weak Hopf quantum double phase
D(W)
are characterized by
W
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}
.
The boundary excitations are described by the category of
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules.
For two gapped boundaries determined by
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
, the point defects between them are classified by the
\mathfrak{B}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules. The results are summarized in Table
1
.

Proof.

This is proved in
(
Henker 2011
, Lemma 10.1.2)
.
∎

For Hopf algebra
W=H
case, we know that for any indecomposable module category
\mathcal{M}
over the representation category
\mathsf{Rep}(H)
of the Hopf algebra, there is a corresponding
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathcal{M}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
.
However, for weak Hopf algebra, this direction is still open.
Nevertheless, for any Morita equivalence of
W
-comodule algebra, we have a corresponding gapped boundary theory.
The main results of this formalism of gapped boundary are summarized in Table
1
.
Similar to what we have done for Hopf quantum double phase, the gapped boundary can also be described by
W
-module algebra. We will not repeat the discussion we gave in
Jia et al. 2022
here and just summarize the results in Table
2
.

5.2
Gapped domain wall

Consider the domain wall that separates two
2d
topological phases with input unitary (multi-) fusion categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
, the domain wall theory is defined by a
\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}
-bimodule category
\mathcal{M}

Kitaev and Kong 2012
;
Fuchs et al. 2013
.
The topological boundary excitations are given by the category of all
\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}
-bimodule functors from
\mathcal{M}
to itself,
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})
.

Here in our case, two weak Hopf quantum double phases are determined by
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathsf{Rep}(W_{i})
,
i=1,2
.
To generalize our previous gapped domain wall theory
Jia et al. 2022
, we introduce the concept of weak Hopf bicomodule algebra. For the weak Hopf case, we need a similar definition. This seems not to be systematically discussed before. In this part, we outline some results, and a more comprehensive and rigorous discussion of weak Hopf bicomodule algebra will be given elsewhere
Jia and Tan tionb
.

Definition 10
.

Let
W_{1},W_{2}
be two weak Hopf algebras. An algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is called a
W_{1}|W_{2}
-bicomodule algebra if
\mathfrak{A}
is a
left
W_{1}
-comodule algebra with comodule map
\alpha
and a right
W_{2}
-comodule algebra with comodule map
\beta
such that
(\mathrm{id}_{W_{1}}\otimes\beta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\alpha=(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W_{2}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\beta
. A bicomodule algebra map is an algebra map intertwining both the left and right comodule maps.

For a given bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, the category
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
of finite dimensional modules is a
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
-bimodule category.
The domain wall excitations are classified by the
\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}
-bimodule functors.

Lemma 6
.

For a
W_{1}|W_{2}
bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, the category
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
of left
\mathfrak{A}
-modules is a
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
-bimodule category.

Proof.

From the discussion in Sec.
5.1
,
X_{1}\otimes M
is a left
\mathfrak{A}
-module for all
X_{1}\in\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})
and
M\in{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
, and the tensor product is defined as the submodule
X_{1}\otimes_{\mathfrak{A}}M
. The associator is defined as the identity, and the unit isomorphism is given by
\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes m_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i}\varepsilon(x_{i})m_{i}
.
Thus
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
is a left
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})
-module.

Similarly,
M\otimes X_{2}
is a left
\mathfrak{A}
-module for
M\in{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
and
X_{2}\in\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
(since
\mathfrak{A}
is a right
W_{2}
-comodule), with the structure map given by (in diagrammatic representation)

(5.3)

The tensor product is defined as the submodule
M\otimes_{\mathfrak{A}}X_{2}
, the associator is the identity, and the unit isomorphism is given by
\sum_{i}m_{i}\otimes y_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i}m_{i}\varepsilon(y_{i})
.
In this way,
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
is a right
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
-module.

For
X_{i}\in\mathsf{Rep}(W_{i})
and
M\in{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
,
X_{1}\otimes M\otimes X_{2}
is also an
\mathfrak{A}
-module.
In fact, it is easy to verify that the module structure map is given by (in diagrammatic representation)

\mu_{X_{1}\otimes M\otimes X_{2}}=\begin{aligned} \hbox to112.4pt{\vbox to117.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.78868pt\lower-78.63156pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{}{}{}}}}{}

(5.4)

The tensor product is defined as
X_{1}\otimes_{\mathfrak{A}}M\otimes_{\mathfrak{A}}X_{2}
. Thus the middle associator is also defined as the identity.
This proves that
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
is a
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
-bimodule category.
∎

Proposition 4
.

The topological excitations of the gapped domain wall determined by a
W_{1}|W_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
are equivalently described by the following categories:

1.

The category of
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
-bimodule functors
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})}({{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod})
.

2.

The category of
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop})
-module functors
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop})}({{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod})
.

3.

The category of all
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Instead of proving the above proposition about topological excitations on the domain wall, we will prove a more general result about the point defects on the domain wall. The topological excitations can be regarded as a special case of point defects.

Proposition 5
.

The point defects between two gapped domain walls determined by
W_{1}|W_{2}
-bicomodule algebras
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
are equivalently described by the following categories:

1.

The category of
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})
-bimodule functors
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2})}({{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod})
.

2.

The category of
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop})
-module functors
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop})}({{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod})
.

3.

The category of all
\mathfrak{B}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Proof.

1
\Leftrightarrow
2: This is clear from the facts that,
\mathsf{Rep}(W^{\rm cop})=\mathsf{Rep}(W)^{\otimes\rm op}
, a
\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}
-bimodule category is equivalent to a left
\mathcal{C}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\rm\otimes\rm op}
-module category, and
\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop})=\mathsf{Rep}(W_{1})\boxtimes\mathsf{Rep}(W_{2}^{\rm cop})
.

2
\Leftrightarrow
3: This is ensured by Eilenberg-Watts theorem
Eilenberg 1960
;
Watts 1960
.
∎

Notice that using the folding trick, a gapped domain wall can be transformed into a gapped boundary. And the gapped boundary can be regarded as a special gapped domain wall that separates the quantum double phase from the trivial phase. Our established theory of boundary and domain wall matches well with this correspondence.

6
Hamiltonian realization of gapped boundaries and domain walls

In this section, let us give the explicit lattice Hamiltonian of the gapped boundary and domain wall of the weak Hopf quantum double model.
We want to stress that various existing boundary Hamiltonian models of the quantum double model are incomplete
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
;
Beigi et al. 2011
;
Cong et al. 2017a
;
Jia et al. 2022
, namely, they cannot cover all possible boundaries.
From our discussion in the previous section, we see that to cover all possible gapped boundaries of the quantum double model
D(W)
, we need to build the boundary Hamiltonian for all
W
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}
such that the boundary excitation realizes the bimodule category
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}
.
For the domain wall, a similar issue exists.
This part will give a complete solution to these problems in the most general settings.

6.1
Gapped boundary Hamiltonian

We have argued that all gapped boundaries of quantum double model
D(W)
are characterized by
W
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}
.
We now construct the corresponding lattice model for a given
\mathfrak{A}
.
Notice that our construction based on the generalized quantum double in
Jia et al. 2022
can be straightforwardly generalized to the weak Hopf quantum double model.
Here, we will present a different but more intuitive construction.

Without loss of generality, we only consider the surface
\Sigma
with one boundary
\partial\Sigma
.
To each bulk edge
e
, we still have
\mathcal{H}_{e}=W
, while for boundary edge
e_{b}
, we set
\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}=\mathfrak{A}
.
Notice that here the boundary edge is directed: if the boundary face
f
is on the left-hand side of
e_{b}
,
\mathfrak{A}
should be chosen as a left
W
-comodule algebra, otherwise, it should be chosen as a right
W
-comodule algebra.
This is because we need to introduce a pairing between
x\in\mathfrak{A}
and
\varphi\in\hat{W}
. When
f
in on the left-hand side of
e_{b}
,
x\leftharpoonup\hat{S}(\phi)
is well defined only when
\mathfrak{A}
is a left
W
-comodule algebra. In this case,
x^{[1]}\in W
, and we have
x\leftharpoonup\hat{S}(\phi)=\sum\varphi(S(x^{[1]}))x^{[0]}
.
Similarly, when
f
is on the right-hand side of
e_{b}
,
\mathfrak{A}
must be a right
W
-comodule algebra.
In the following, we will assume that the boundary direction is chosen so that the bulk is always on the right-hand side of the boundary, thus we assign all boundary edges the right
W
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.

To build the boundary model, we need the notion of
symmetric separability idempotent
of an algebra
\mathfrak{A}
(see
Aguiar 2000
;
Koppen 2020
).
By definition, it is an element
\lambda=\sum_{<\lambda>}\lambda^{<1>}\otimes\lambda^{<2>}\in\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}
such that

1.

\sum_{<\lambda>}x\lambda^{<1>}\otimes\lambda^{<2>}=\sum_{<\lambda>}\lambda^{<1>}\otimes\lambda^{<2>}x
for all
x\in\mathfrak{A}
;

2.

\sum_{<\lambda>}\lambda^{<1>}\lambda^{<2>}=1
;

3.

\sum_{<\lambda>}\lambda^{<1>}\otimes\lambda^{<2>}=\sum_{<\lambda>}\lambda^{<2>}\otimes\lambda^{<1>}
.

It can be proved that
\lambda
is an idempotent of the enveloping algebra
\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}
.
If
K
is a weak Hopf algebra with Haar integral
h_{K}
, it is easy to check that
\lambda=\sum_{(h_{K})}h_{K}^{(1)}\otimes S(h_{K}^{(2)})
is a symmetric separability idempotent.
The existence and uniqueness of the symmetric separability idempotent for a finite-dimensional semisimple algebra over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero are proved in
(
Aguiar 2000
, Corollary 3.1)
.
It is also proved that
(
Koppen 2020
, Proposition 19)

\sum\lambda^{<1>[0]}\otimes\lambda^{<2>[0]}\otimes\lambda^{<1>[1]}\lambda^{<2>[1]}=\sum\lambda^{<1>}\otimes\lambda^{<2>}\otimes 1,

(6.1)

that is,
\sum\beta(\lambda^{<1>})_{13}\beta(\lambda^{<2>})_{23}=\lambda_{12}
in
\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}\otimes W
.

For each boundary edge
e_{b}
, we propose the following edge operator
E^{z\otimes w}_{e_{b}}
for
z\otimes w\in\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}
:

E^{z\otimes w}_{e_{b}}|x\rangle=|zxw\rangle.

(6.2)

For general
\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}
, using linear expansion and the above expression we can define
E^{\alpha}_{e_{b}}
.
It is clear that

E^{z\otimes w}_{e_{b}}E^{g\otimes k}_{e_{b}}=E^{zg\otimes kw}_{e_{b}}.

(6.3)

Thus it forms a representation of the enveloping algebra
\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}
of
\mathfrak{A}
.

Let
\lambda
be a symmetric separability idempotent. Then

E^{\lambda}_{e_{b}}|x\rangle=\sum_{<\lambda>}|\lambda^{<1>}x\lambda^{<2>}\rangle

(6.4)

is the boundary local stabilizer that we need to construct the boundary lattice model.
From the definition of
\lambda
, we see that
(E^{\lambda}_{e_{b}})^{2}=E^{\lambda}_{e_{b}}
.

Another local operator we need is the boundary vertex operator.
Since we assume that the direction of all boundary edges is chosen so that the bulk is on the right-hand side of the boundary edge, there are only two configurations depending on the bulk edges connecting to the boundary vertex.
More precisely, we define the following operator for
z\otimes w\in\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\quad A^{z\otimes w}(s_{b})|x,y,h\rangle=\sum_{[z]}|z^{[0]}x,yw,hS^{-1}(z^{[1]})\rangle.

(6.8)

The convention here follows from Eqs. (
4.1
)-(
4.8
).
It is clear that the
A_{v_{b}}^{z\otimes w}
’s form a representation of
\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}
.
The local stabilizers are defined as
A^{\lambda}(s_{b})
with
\lambda
the symmetric separability idempotent, and
B_{f_{b}}=B^{\varphi_{\hat{W}}}(s_{b})
.
The boundary Hamiltonian is thus of the form

H[C(\partial\Sigma)]=-\sum_{s_{b}}A^{\lambda}({s_{b}})-\sum_{f_{b}}B_{f_{b}}.

(6.9)

Notice that for the gapped boundary determined by a weak Hopf subalgebra
K\subseteq W
,
K
is naturally a
W
-comodule algebra.
Using the connection between symmetric separability idempotent
\lambda
and Haar integral
h
, i.e.,
\lambda=\sum_{(h)}h^{(1)}\otimes S(h^{(2)})
, we can obtain the counterpart of the model given in our previous work
Jia et al. 2022
for Hopf algebra quantum double boundary.

Remark 6.1
.

Compare this with the construction given in
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
;
Cong et al. 2017a
, where they try to introduce the local boundary edge term and local boundary face and vertex terms to model the gapped boundary.
Our model here is simpler and more natural in our framework since the
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodule structure is encoded in a way just as that for the bulk quantum double on a given site.
The philosophy is also different. In their construction, they are searching for a local algebra
\mathcal{A}
such that the boundary excitation is just the category of the representation category
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathcal{A})
.
In the algebraic framework we established in the last section, we see that the boundary excitation is just the
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodule category
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}
. Our construction fits better into our framework.

Remark 6.2
.

For the group quantum double boundary, the construction given by Beigi et al.
Beigi et al. 2011
can be recovered in our framework.
Notice that in that case, the boundary is determined by a subgroup
K\subset G
.

\mathfrak{A}=\mathbb{C}[K]
is a
W=\mathbb{C}[G]
-comodule algebra.
The local stabilizer
A^{K}=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k\in K}A^{k}
.
Using the fact that symmetric separability idempotent
\lambda=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k\in K}k\otimes S(k)
, it is clear that
A^{K}=A^{\lambda}_{v_{b}}
.

Remark 6.3
.

Here we choose the comodule algebra formalism of gapped boundary. For the module algebra description, we have a similar construction. The only thing that needs to be changed is that in the module algebra case, the boundary local stabilizer is built from symmetric separability idempotents.

6.2
Gapped domain wall Hamiltonian

As we have pointed out before, a domain wall can be understood equivalently as a boundary via the folding trick.
Nevertheless, it is worth spelling out the explicit data for the construction of a gapped domain wall.
The domain wall between
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
is equivalent to a boundary of
W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop}
.
Thus the domain wall is given by a
W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop}
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.
Choose the symmetric separability idempotent
\lambda
, we can construct a corresponding
\mathfrak{A}
-boundary of
W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop}
phase.
See from domain wall settings, we have a left
W_{1}
- right
W_{2}
- comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.
We construct the domain wall vertex operator
A^{z\otimes w}(s_{d,i})

as follows (for clarity, we have changed the tensor product order when necessary):

\displaystyle\quad\quad A^{z\otimes w}(s_{d,i})|x,k,y,h\rangle=\sum_{[w]}|zx,S^{-1}(w^{[-1]})k,yw^{[0]},S^{-1}(w^{[1]})h\rangle,

(6.16)

\displaystyle\quad\quad A^{z\otimes w}(s_{d,i})|x,k,y,h\rangle=\sum_{[z]}|z^{[0]}x,z^{[-1]}k,yw,z^{[1]}h\rangle.

(6.17)

Notice that in the above expressions, we assume that
k\in W_{1}
,
h\in W_{2}
and
x,y,z,w\in\mathfrak{A}
.
The local stabilizers are defined as
A^{\lambda}(s_{d,i})
and
B_{f_{d,i}}
(
i=1,2
), and the domain wall Hamiltonian is

H[C(\Sigma_{d})]=-\sum_{i=1,2}\sum_{s_{d,i}}A^{\lambda}(s_{d_{i}})-\sum_{i=1,2}\sum_{f_{d,i}}B_{f_{d,i}}.

(6.18)

If one of the two bulks is set as a trivial phase, the domain wall reduces to a boundary.

7
Weak Hopf tensor network states

In this section, we shall introduce the weak Hopf tensor network based on pairings and solve the weak Hopf quantum double model with weak Hopf tensor network states.
Our discussion here inherits the philosophy of the construction in
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
;
Girelli et al. 2021
;
Jia et al. 2022
, that is, introducing face and edge labels and pairing the face labels with edge labels. Although the construction is similar, the proof is more complicated in this situation.
There are also alternative approaches to the topological tensor network which is mainly based on the vertex freedom
Schuch et al. 2010
;
Lootens et al. 2021
;
Molnar et al. 2022
. If we consider the dual lattice model, our tensor network will share some similarities with these vertex-based constructions.

7.1
Weak Hopf tensor networks

Let us first introduce the weak Hopf tensor network and then in the sequel subsections, we show how to use this tool to solve the weak Hopf quantum double model. The construction is similar to that of
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
;
Jia et al. 2022
, however, as we will see, the calculation and proof are more complicated in this case. The results here can also be generalized to the semidual case
Girelli et al. 2021
.

Local rank-2 tensor from the pairing of weak Hopf algebras.
—  Our construction of the weak Hopf tensor network is based on pairings between weak Hopf algebras.
Let
J,W
be two weak Hopf algebras equipped with a pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:J\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
.
The basic building block of the weak Hopf tensor network is the rank-2 tensor
\Psi(x,\phi)=\langle\phi,x\rangle
with
x\in W,\phi\in J
, which is represented by

(7.2)

The black edge is labeled with
x\in W
and the reversing of the edge is realized by applying the antipode of
W
on
x
.
Similarly, the blue circular edge is labeled with
\phi\in J
and the reversing of the edge is realized by applying the antipode of
J
on
\phi
.
The Hopf tensor network representation is a diagrammatic representation of the pairing. For arbitrary given rank-2 tensors with their corresponding label, the evaluation is determined by pairing, and this process will be called Hopf trace.

Gluing Local rank-2 tensors.
—  Using the above local rank-2 tensor, we can construct a topological tensor network by introducing the gluing process of these local tensors.
Consider two weak Hopf algebras
J
and
I
which both have their pairings with weak Hopf algebra
W
, we can contract the rank-2 tensors to obtain rank-3 tensors.
There are two types of basic gluing processes. The first one is parallel gluing, for which the contraction is determined by the coproduct of
W
.
For example, let
\phi\in J
,
\psi\in I
and
x\in W
, we define

{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.8}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.8}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -15.75 -15.75 M -15.75 -15.75 L -15.75 15.75 L 15.75 15.75 L 15.75 -15.75 Z M 15.75 15.75}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -39.37 0 L 34.39 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{24.85281pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.98 0 C 3.51 0.28 1.11 1.11 -0.55 2.08 L -0.55 -2.08 C 1.11 -1.11 3.51 -0.28 4.98 0}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(7.3)

The second one is vertical gluing, for which the contraction is determined by the coproduct of
J
. For example, let
\phi\in J
and
x,y\in W
, we have

(7.4)

Figure 2:
Example of the weak Hopf tensor network on a square lattice. The black lattice represents the physical lattice, the blue circle in the face
f
is labeled with a face element
\phi_{f}\in J_{f}
, and the red box on the edge is labeled with an edge element
g\in W
. There is a pairing between
J
and
W
.

Weak Hopf tensor network on a lattice.
—   With the above preparation, we are now ready to introduce the weak Hopf tensor network on a lattice
\Gamma
.
We assign a weak Hopf algebra
W_{e}
to each edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
and a weak Hopf algebra
J_{f}
to each face
f\in F(\Gamma)
.
For an edge
e
, two face algebras
J_{f_{L}}
and
J_{f_{R}}
neighboring
e
have their corresponding pairings with
W_{e}
.
There is one more extra data necessary. When dealing with a face, the starting site determines the starting point of the ordered pairing.
See Fig.
2
, where the starting point is drawn as a blue dot. The evaluation rule is given as follows:

\begin{aligned} \hbox to86.05pt{\vbox to91.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.02138pt\lower-16.8667pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.97604pt}{26.29999pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{
