{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-36.68837pt}{26.29999pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.56 11.81 M 30.54 11.81 C 30.54 13.46 29.2 14.79 27.56 14.79 C 25.91 14.79 24.58 13.46 24.58 11.81 C 24.58 10.17 25.91 8.83 27.56 8.83 C 29.2 8.83 30.54 10.17 30.54 11.81 Z M 27.56 11.81}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}=\sum_{(\phi)}\begin{aligned} \langle\phi^{(1)},x\rangle\langle\phi^{(2)},S(y)\rangle\langle\phi^{(3)},S(z)\rangle\langle\phi^{(4)},w\rangle.\end{aligned}

(7.5)

In this way, for a lattice
\Gamma
with all edges labeled with
g_{e}
and faces labeled with
\phi_{f}
, we can evaluate it and obtain a tensor

\Psi_{\Gamma}(\{x_{e}\}_{e\in E(\Gamma)},\{\phi_{f}\}_{f\in F(\Gamma)})=\mathrm{ttr}_{\Gamma}(\{x_{e}\}_{e\in E_{\Gamma}},\{\phi_{f}\}_{f\in F_{\Gamma}}),

(7.6)

where
\operatorname{ttr}_{\Gamma}
is called the weak Hopf trace over the lattice
\Gamma
.

Hierarchy.
—  Similar to the Hopf algebra case, if we choose a pair of weak Hopf algebras
J\subset\hat{W}
and
K\subset W
, we obtain the solution of the Hamiltonian constructed from
J^{\rm cop}\Join K
.

7.2
Ground state of weak Hopf quantum double model

Let us now define the quantum states that are determined by a weak Hopf tensor network.
Consider a lattice
C(\Sigma)
of a
2d
surface
\Sigma
with face set
F
and edge set
E
. We assign to each face
f
a weak Hopf algebra
J_{f}
and each edge
e
a weak Hopf algebra
W_{e}
.
Assume that for each face,
J_{f}
has the corresponding pairings with the edge Hopf algebras for edges
e\in\partial f
.
If we set the edge with values
x_{e}\in W_{e}
and face with values
\phi_{f}\in J_{f}
, then we obtain a weak Hopf tensor network

(\otimes_{e\in E}x_{e})\otimes(\otimes_{f\in F}\phi_{f})\mapsto\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\})=\mathrm{ttr}_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\}).

(7.7)

The corresponding weak Hopf tensor network states are defined as

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\})\rangle=\sum_{(x_{e})}\mathrm{ttr}_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}^{(2)}\},\{\phi_{f}\})\otimes_{e\in E}|x_{e}^{(1)}\rangle.

(7.8)

Ground state of weak Hopf quantum double model.
​—
Let us now give the explicit expression of the ground state of the weak Hopf quantum double model using the weak Hopf tensor network state.
In this case, all edge weak Hopf algebras are set as
W
, and all face weak Hopf algebras are set as
\hat{W}
.
We assign Haar integrals to edges and faces, then from Eq. (
7.8
) we obtain a quantum state, which is to be proved the ground state of the weak Hopf quantum double model.
The depiction of local tensor networks for face and vertex are given in Fig.
3
.

Theorem 4
.

Consider a given lattice
C(\Sigma)
and the corresponding quantum double model
H[C(\Sigma)]
. If we assign each edge with Haar integral
h_{W}
and each face with Haar integral
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, then the corresponding weak Hopf tensor network state

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{h_{e}=h_{W}\}_{e},\{\phi_{f}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}\}_{f})\rangle

(7.9)

is the ground state of
H[C(\Sigma)]
.

Figure 3:
The depiction of face and vertex of the weak Hopf tensor network representation of the ground state of the weak Hopf quantum double model.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, consider the vertex
v
in Fig.
3
. Since
A_{v}
only acts non-trivially on all edges attaching to
v
, it suffices to consider its behavior around
v
. Label the edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}
counterclockwise starting from the rightmost edge, and denote
h_{j}=h_{W},\,\varphi_{j}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}
for
j=1,2,3,4
. Near this vertex, the state
|\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{h_{e}=h_{W}\}_{e},\{\phi_{f}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}\}_{f})\rangle
is given by

\displaystyle|\Psi_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

(7.10)

\displaystyle\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle.

For
h=h_{W}
, using
S(h^{(i)})=S^{-1}(h^{(i)})
, one computes

\displaystyle A_{v}|\Psi_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad|h_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)}),h_{2}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}h_{3}^{(1)},h^{(4)}h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}h^{(2)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(3)}),h^{(4)}h_{3}^{(1)},h^{(5)}h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h^{(4)}h_{3}^{(1)},h^{(5)}h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})h^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h^{(4)}h_{3}^{(1)},h^{(5)}h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(S(h^{(3)})h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h^{(4)}h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(S(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})h^{(3)})h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h^{(4)}h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(S(h^{(3)})h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\sum_{(h)}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(S(h^{(3)})S(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)}))h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle.

Here, the fourth, sixth and eighth equalities follow from
\sum_{(y)}xS(y^{(1)})\otimes y^{(2)}=\sum_{(y)}S(y^{(1)})\otimes y^{(2)}x
and
\sum_{(y)}y^{(1)}\otimes xy^{(2)}=\sum_{(y)}S(x)y^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}
for
x\in W_{L}
. We show how to deduce the second equality, and the remaining equalities are obtained similarly. Indeed, the second equality is from the following computation:

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{(h_{1})}\Delta(h_{1}^{(2)})\otimes|h_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{1})}\sum_{(h^{(1)})}\Delta(h_{1}^{(2)})\otimes|S^{-1}(h^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(1)}))S(h_{1}^{(1)}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{1})}\sum_{(h^{(1)})}\Delta(h_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(1)})))\otimes|h_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{1})}\sum_{(h^{(1)})}\Delta(h_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(2)})h^{(1)})\otimes|h_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{1})}\sum_{(h^{(1)})}\Delta(h_{1}^{(2)}\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(2)}))h^{(1)})\otimes|h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{1})}\Delta(h_{1}^{(2)}h^{(1)})\otimes|h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle,

where the fourth equality comes from
h_{1}S^{-1}(h^{(2)})=h_{1}\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(2)}))
.

Next, consider the face in Fig.
3
. In the same spirit, the state
|\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{h_{e}=h_{W}\}_{e},\{\phi_{f}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}\}_{f})\rangle
is given, near this face, by

\displaystyle|\Psi_{f}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi_{1}(S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)}))

(7.11)

\displaystyle\times\varphi(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)})S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

where
\varphi=\varphi_{j}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}
,
j=1,2,3,4
. For
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, one computes

\displaystyle B_{f}|\Psi_{f}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi(h_{1}^{(4)}S(h_{2}^{(3)})S(h_{3}^{(3)})h_{4}^{(4)})\varphi^{\prime}(S(h_{1}^{(1)})h_{2}^{(2)}h_{3}^{(2)}S(h_{4}^{(1)}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{1}(S(h_{1}^{(3)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(4)})\varphi_{3}(h_{3}^{(4)})\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(3)}))|h_{1}^{(2)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi(h_{1}^{(1)}S(h_{2}^{(3)})S(h_{3}^{(3)})h_{4}^{(1)})\varphi^{\prime}(h_{1}^{(2)}S(h_{2}^{(2)})S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{1}(S(h_{1}^{(4)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(4)})\varphi_{3}(h_{3}^{(4)})\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(4)}))|h_{1}^{(3)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(3)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi^{2}(h_{1}^{(1)}S(h_{2}^{(2)})S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(1)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{1}(S(h_{1}^{(3)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(3)}))|h_{1}^{(2)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{j})}\varphi(h_{1}^{(3)}S(h_{2}^{(2)})S(h_{3}^{(2)})h_{4}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\varphi_{1}(S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{(3)})\varphi_{3}(h_{3}^{(3)})\varphi_{4}(S(h_{4}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)},h_{4}^{(1)}\rangle,

by
\varphi^{2}=\varphi
. This completes the proof.
∎

Ground states of the boundary.
—
We now solve the boundary ground state.
Let
(C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma),C(\partial\Sigma))
be a lattice on a
2d
surface
\Sigma
with boundary
\partial\Sigma
. Consider the boundary model determined by a right
W
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.
The Hopf tensor network states of the boundary model are defined as

\displaystyle|\Psi_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma),C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{g_{e}\},\{\varphi_{f}\},\{x_{e_{b}}\},\{\phi_{f_{b}}\})\rangle

(7.12)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g_{e}),(x_{e_{b}})}\operatorname{ttr}_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma),C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{g_{e}^{(2)}\},\{\varphi_{f}\},\{x_{e_{b}}^{[1]}\},\{\phi_{f_{b}}\})

\displaystyle\otimes_{e\in E(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)}g_{e}^{(1)}\otimes_{e_{b}\in E(\partial\Sigma)}x_{e_{b}}^{[0]},

where
g_{e}\in W,\,\varphi_{f}\in\hat{W},\,x_{e_{b}}\in\mathfrak{A}
, and
\phi_{f_{b}}\in\hat{W}
.

For simplicity, let us consider the case
\mathfrak{A}=K
where
K
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of
W
. In this case, the symmetric separability idempotent is
\lambda=\sum_{(h)}h^{(1)}\otimes S(h^{(2)})
with
h=h_{K}
the Haar integral of
K
. If we assign each bulk edge with Haar integral
h_{W}
, each face with Haar integral
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, and each boundary edge
h_{K}
, then the corresponding weak Hopf tensor network state

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma),C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{g_{e}=h_{W}\}_{e},\{\varphi_{f}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}\}_{f},\{x_{e_{b}}=h_{K}\}_{e_{b}},\{\phi_{f_{b}}=\varphi_{\hat{W}}\}_{f_{b}})\rangle

(7.13)

is the ground state of the quantum double model with boundary
H[C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma),C(\partial\Sigma)]
. To see this, consider the boundary vertex
v_{b}
in Eq. (
6.5
), over which the state (
7.13
) is given by

|\Psi_{v_{b}}\rangle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{W}^{(2)}S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(S(h_{W}^{(3)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}\rangle,

(7.14)

with
h_{1}=h_{2}=h_{K}
. It suffices to show that
|\Psi_{v_{b}}\rangle
is preserved by the boundary stabilizer
A^{\lambda}_{v_{b}}
. In the following computation, we used repeatedly the fact that
S(h^{(i)})=S^{-1}(h^{(i)})
for Haar integral
h
. One computes

\displaystyle A^{\lambda}_{v_{b}}|\Psi_{v_{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{W}^{(2)}S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(S(h_{W}^{(3)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))|h^{(1)}h_{1}^{(1)},h^{(2)}h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}S(h^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{W}^{(2)}S(S(h^{(1)})h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(S(h_{W}^{(3)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h^{(2)}h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}S(h^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(S(h^{(3)})h_{W}^{(2)}S(h_{1}^{(2)})h^{(1)})\varphi_{2}(S(S(h^{(2)})h_{W}^{(3)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}S(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{W}^{(2)}S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(S(S(h^{(1)})h_{W}^{(3)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}S(h^{(2)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{W}^{(2)}S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(S(h_{W}^{(3)})h^{(1)}S(h_{2}^{(2)}h^{(2)})|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{W}^{(2)}S(h_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi_{2}(S(h_{W}^{(3)})S(h_{2}^{(2)}))|h_{1}^{(1)},h_{W}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)}\rangle.

Here, the second equality holds as

\displaystyle\sum|h_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|h^{(1)}h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|h_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|h^{(1)}S(S(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(2)})))h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|S(\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(2)}))h_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|h^{(1)}h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|S(h^{(3)})h^{(2)}h_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|h^{(1)}h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|S(h^{(2)})h_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|S(h^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)})\rangle\otimes|\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})S(h_{1}^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|S(h^{(2)})S(h_{1}^{(2)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)}))\rangle\otimes|S(h_{1}^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum|S(h^{(1)})h_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|h_{1}^{(1)}\rangle.

The third and fifth equalities follow from similar computations. The trick is similar to the proof of Theorem
4
.

Ground states of the domain wall.
—  Similarly, we can solve the domain wall ground state. For a
W_{1}|W_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, denote by
\alpha(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{[-1]}\otimes x^{[0]}\in W_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{A}
and by
\beta(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{[0]}\otimes x^{[1]}\in\mathfrak{A}\otimes W_{2}
,
x\in\mathfrak{A}
, respectively the left
W_{1}
- and right
W_{2}
- coactions as usual. So
(\mathrm{id}_{W_{1}}\otimes\beta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\alpha(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{[-1]}\otimes x^{[0]}\otimes x^{[1]}
is well defined. For a domain wall determined by
\mathfrak{A}
, the Hopf tensor network states are defined as

\displaystyle|\Psi_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{g_{e_{1}}\},\{\varphi_{f_{1}}\},\{\phi_{f_{d,1}}\},\{x_{e_{d}}\},\{\phi_{f_{d,2}}\},\{g_{e_{2}}\},\{\varphi_{f_{2}}\})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g_{e_{i}}),(x_{e_{b}})}\operatorname{ttr}_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{g_{e_{1}}^{(2)}\},\{\varphi_{f_{1}}\},\{x_{e_{d}}^{[-1]}\},\{\phi_{f_{d,1}}\},\{x_{e_{d}}^{[1]}\},\{\phi_{f_{d,2}}\},\{g_{e_{2}}^{(2)}\},\{\varphi_{f_{2}}\})\rangle

\displaystyle\otimes_{e_{1}\in E(\Sigma_{1})}g_{e}^{(1)}\otimes_{e_{d}\in E(\Sigma_{d})}x_{e_{d}}^{[0]}\otimes_{e_{2}\in E(\Sigma_{2})}g_{e_{2}}^{(1)},

where
g_{e_{1}}\in W_{1},\,\varphi_{f_{1}},\,\phi_{f_{d,1}}\in\hat{W}_{1},\,g_{e_{2}}\in W_{2},\,\varphi_{f_{2}},\,\phi_{f_{d,2}}\in\hat{W}_{2}
, and
x_{e_{d}}\in\mathfrak{A}
.

Consider the case that
\mathfrak{A}=K
is a weak Hopf subalgebra of
W_{1}\otimes W_{2}^{\rm cop}
. Then one can use a similar computation as in the boundary to show that the ground state of the domain wall is given by the weak Hopf tensor network state

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{h_{W_{1}}\}_{e_{1}},\{\varphi_{\hat{W}_{1}}\}_{f_{1}},\{h_{K}\}_{e_{d}},\{\varphi_{\hat{W}_{1}}\}_{f_{d,1}},\{\varphi_{\hat{W}_{2}}\}_{f_{d,2}},\{h_{W_{2}}\}_{e_{2}},\{\varphi_{\hat{W}_{2}}\}_{f_{2}})\rangle.

7.3
Closed ribbon operator and ground state degeneracy

We have shown that the ground state of the quantum double model can be described as a weak Hopf tensor network state. However, the ground state space is degenerate for general closed surfaces. We now address the question of generating additional ground states from the expression in Eq. (
7.9
).
This can be achieved via the action of a closed ribbon operator.

Let us take the type-B ribbon as an example. As discussed before, we have the decomposition of ribbon operator algebra
D(W)^{\vee}
as in Eq. (
4.56
).
Consider the projection
\operatorname{Proj}_{X}D(W)^{\vee}\cong X\otimes X^{\vee}
.
For a closed ribbon
\rho
with
\partial_{0}\rho=\partial_{1}\rho=s
, the particle
X
and
X^{\vee}
fuse to vacuum
\mathds{1}
at site
s
.
Consider the projection of
X\otimes X^{\vee}\to\mathds{1}
, this can also be embedded into
D(W)
via

\mathds{1}\hookrightarrow X\otimes X^{\vee}\hookrightarrow D(W)^{\vee}.

(7.15)

We denote the corresponding image as
\operatorname{Proj}_{\mathds{1},X}D(W)^{\vee}
.
For closed ribbon
\rho
and
g\otimes\psi\in\operatorname{Proj}_{\mathds{1},X}D(W)^{\vee}
we have the ribbon operator
F^{g,\psi}_{\rho}
.
Now for a closed surface
\Sigma
with the cellulation
C(\Sigma)
and weak Hopf quantum double model
H[D(W),C(\Sigma)]
, using the expression in Eq. (
7.9
), we obtain a ground state
|\Omega\rangle
.
Then for a closed ribbon
\rho
,
F_{\rho}^{g,\psi}|\Omega\rangle
is also a ground state.
Depending on the topology of the surface
\Sigma
, there may be non-homotopical closed ribbons, for these ribbons, applying the ribbon operator above, we will obtain different ground states in the vacuum sector
\mathcal{V}_{\mathds{1}}
.

8
Topological duality of weak Hopf quantum double phase

One crucial property of the weak Hopf quantum double model is that they have EM duality. Mathematically, this stems from the fact that weak Hopf algebras are self-dual, that is, when
W
is a weak Hopf algebra, its dual algebra
\hat{W}
is also a weak Hopf algebra. And the representation categories of
W
and
\hat{W}
are categorical Morita equivalent.
This EM duality is important to understand the matter phases and phase transitions. It is proved that the EM duality is closely related to the well-known Kramers-Wannier duality of Ising model
Freed and Teleman 2022
;
Aasen et al. 2016
;
Aasen et al. 2020
.

We know that the Abelian group quantum double model has EM duality, under which the gauge charges and gauge fluxes are exchanged in the dual theory.
The corresponding dual group is
\hat{G}
which consists of all irreducible representations of
G
.
Since
G\cong\hat{G}
, the topological phases for the two theories are equivalent.
However, when
G
is non-Abelian, the EM duality is broken, since the irreducible representations of
G
do not form a group.
One approach to remedy this problem is to generalize the EM duality as a partial EM duality
Hu and Wan 2020
, i.e., consider a normal subgroup
N\subset G
, the
N
-charge and
N
-flux are exchanged under the partial EM duality.
Another way is to generalize the notion of group symmetry to the quantum group symmetry (like the Hopf algebras
Buerschaper et al. 2013b
and the weak Hopf algebras discussed in this work).
In this case, the input data of the dual model is the dual Hopf algebra.
Two corresponding quantum double phases are equivalent.
The EM duality of the group quantum double model in the presence of a gapped boundary and domain wall is also discussed previously
Wang et al. 2020
;
Jia and Kaszlikowski 2022
;
Kitaev and Kong 2012
. Under the bulk EM duality, the boundary types are permuted accordingly.

In this part, we consider the most general case where the EM duality can be realized,
viz.
, the weak Hopf quantum double model without and with the gapped boundary or domain wall.
Besides EM duality, we will also define the general concept of duality which connects two equivalent quantum double phases.

8.1
Topological duality for the
2d
bulk

The duality is a correspondence between two physically equivalent theories. Before we discuss the duality, let us first consider the general map between two anomaly-free topological phases
8
8

8

The phases that have lattice realizations in the same spatial dimension
Kong and Wen 2014
.
.
Assume that the topological excitations of two phases are given by UMTCs
\mathcal{P}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and their lattice realizations are
H[C_{i}(\Sigma)]
,
i=1,2
(the Hamiltonians are built from two different cellulations of the same surface
\Sigma
).
At excitation level, the general duality map
F:\mathcal{P}_{1}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}
should satisfy the following natural conditions:

1.

The vacuum should be mapped to vacuum
r_{F}:F(\mathds{1})\cong\mathds{1}
.

2.

It preserves the fusion relation
a_{X,Y,F}:F(X\otimes Y)\cong F(X)\otimes F(Y)
. This means that fusion multiplicity is preserved under the duality map.

3.

It preserves the axioms of fusion relations. This means that the
F
-symbol is preserved under the duality map.

4.

Since the direct sum is indeed equivalent to the quantum superposition in the fusion/splitting channel, the duality map should also preserve the direct sum structure.
That is,
F
should map zero objects to zero objects and
F(X\oplus Y)\cong F(X)\oplus F(Y)
, and the axioms of direct sum structure is also preserved.

5.

When acting on the fusion/splitting space, it is a linear map.

6.

It preserves the braiding structure
F(c_{X,Y})\simeq c_{F(X),F(Y)}
. This means that the duality map preserves the mutual statistics of particles.

At the lattice level, there should be a map
U_{F}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
which maps local terms in the Hamiltonian in a way consistent with the map of topological charge.
A duality between two theories means that the map is bijective, namely,
F
is an equivalence between two UMTCs and
U_{F}
is a Fourier transform between two lattice models.

Based on the above discussion, for quantum double phases, a general map
\Phi
between two weak Hopf quantum double models consists of the following data: (i) A braided monoidal functor
F:\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))\to\mathsf{Rep}(D(W^{\prime}))
; (ii) A Fourier transform
U_{F}
between lattice models
H[D(W);C(\Sigma)]
and
H[D(W^{\prime});C(\Sigma^{\prime})]
. We simply denote it as

\Phi:\mathsf{QD}(D(W);\Sigma)\to\mathsf{QD}(D(W^{\prime});\Sigma^{\prime}).

(8.1)

The braided monoidal functor means that the physical data, such as fusion and braiding, are preserved by the transformation.
The map between two lattice models is a lattice realization of the map between two topological phases.
In this framework, we can define the duality between quantum double phases as follows:

Definition 11
.

A duality between two quantum double phases is an equivalence

\Phi:\mathsf{QD}(D(W);\Sigma)\to\mathsf{QD}(D(W^{\prime});\Sigma^{\prime}),

(8.2)

which consists of a braided monoidal equivalence
F:\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))\to\mathsf{Rep}(D(W^{\prime}))
and an invertible map
U_{F}
between two lattice realizations.

The EM duality is a special case that relates the quantum double model constructed from
W
and that constructed from its dual
\hat{W}
:

\Phi_{\rm EM}:\mathsf{QD}(D(W);\Sigma)\to\mathsf{QD}(D(\hat{W});\Sigma^{\prime}).

(8.3)

This is guaranteed by the fact that there is a braided monoidal equivalence between
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
and
\mathsf{Rep}(D(\hat{W}))
.
And we can also map the lattice model
H(D(W),\Sigma)
to
H(D(\hat{W}),\Sigma^{\prime})
by sending the direct lattice
\Sigma
to its dual lattice
\Sigma^{\prime}=\tilde{\Sigma}
, changing the edge space via Fourier transform between
W
and
\hat{W}
, and exchanging the vertex and face operators with each other (thus the charge and flux are exchanged).
Since
W^{\vee\vee}\cong W
, we see that this map is invertible.

For the more general duality of the weak Hopf quantum double model, we need a notion of categorical Morita equivalence: two monoidal categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are called categorical Morita equivalent
9
9

9

Also called weakly Morita equivalent by some authors
Müger 2003
.
if their Drinfeld centers are braided monoidal equivalent
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D})
.
For two weak Hopf gauge symmetries
W,W^{\prime}
, their representation categories are categorical Morita equivalent if and only if
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(W))\simeq\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(W^{\prime}))
.
From the discussion in Sec.
2
, we know that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(W))
is equivalent to the Yetter-Drinfeld module category
{{}_{W}}\mathsf{YD}^{W}
as a braided monoidal category, and
{{}_{W}}\mathsf{YD}^{W}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
as a braided monoidal category. Therefore, we have:
two quantum double phases with their respective weak Hopf gauge symmetry
W,W^{\prime}
are equivalent (i.e., there is a duality between them)
if and only if
\mathsf{Rep}(W)
and
\mathsf{Rep}(W^{\prime})
are categorical Morita equivalent,
viz.
,
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(W))\simeq\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(W^{\prime}))
.

Remark 8.1
.

The above theorem also holds for general
2d
topological order.
There is a duality between two topological orders with UFC symmetry
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
if and only if
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D})
.
One way to understand this duality is to treat both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
as boundary theories of a
3d
topological order
\mathcal{P}
.
Then using the boundary-bulk correspondence, the boundary anyons of one boundary can be dragged into the bulk, then it can be condensed into another boundary, which realizes the duality between two boundary topological orders.

Remark 8.2

(Duality of Levin-Wen string-net model)
.

The Levin-Wen string-net model is a more general lattice realization of non-chiral anomaly-free topological phase
Levin and Wen 2005b
.
The input data of the model is a UFC
\mathcal{C}
, and the topological excitation is given by the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
.
The general theory of duality in this case can be understood via the module category over
\mathcal{C}
.
It is proved that
Etingof et al. 2010
;
Etingof et al. 2016
for any
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
, the category of
\mathcal{C}
-module functors
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\vee}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})^{\otimes\rm op}
is categorical Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
.
Thus there is a duality

\Phi:\mathsf{SN}(\mathcal{C},H_{\rm SN}[\mathcal{C},C(\Sigma)])\to\mathsf{SN}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\vee},H_{\rm SN}[\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\vee},C(\Sigma)]);

(8.4)

then for different
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, there is also a duality between
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}^{\vee}
theories. Notice that in the string-net model, we cannot define the EM duality, since the Hilbert space is not large enough. To remedy this, we need to introduce the extended string-net model by extending the Hilbert space to support the dyonic excitations
Hu et al. 2018
;
Buerschaper et al. 2013b
.

For weak Hopf symmetry, since there are more structures in
W
than
\mathsf{Rep}(W)
that we can use (actually, via the Tannaka–Krein duality, by introducing a fiber functor to
\mathsf{Rep}(W)
, we can recover
W
from the fiber functor). Thus there are more equivalent ways to explicitly express the duality between quantum double models.
Let us take Hopf algebra and its twist deformation as an example to illustrate this.

Example: the duality of Hopf quantum double model.
—  Consider the Hopf quantum double model
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
;
Jia et al. 2022
, the duality is equivalent to the twist deformation of quantum double
10
10

10

Notice that this is different from the notion of twisted quantum double, which is a variation of the quantum double induced by a
3
-cocycle
\alpha:W\otimes W\otimes W\to U(1)
.
. Namely,
for any two Hopf gauge symmetries
W,W^{\prime}
, their corresponding quantum double phases are equivalent (i.e., there is a duality between them) if and only if there exists a Drinfeld twist
T
such that
D(W)\cong D(W^{\prime})^{T}
as Hopf algebras.
In fact, it is proved
(
Etingof et al. 2016
, Proposition 5.14.4)
that the representation categories of two Hopf algebras are equivalent if and only two Hopf algebras are twist equivalent. Apply this result to quantum double and using the definition of duality, we arrive at the conclusion.

The theorem tells us that the duality between two Hopf quantum double models is fully characterized by a twist.
By definition a twist of a Hopf algebra
W
is an invertible element
T\in W\otimes W
such that

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(T)(T\otimes 1)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(T)(1\otimes T).

(8.5)

The twist can be normalized to ensure that
(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})(T)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)(T)=1
. We will adopt shorthand notation
T=T^{<1>}\otimes T^{<2>}
and
T^{-1}=T^{-<1>}\otimes T^{-<2>}
(similar to the Einstein summation notation, we omit the summation symbol).
We denote
Q_{T}=S(T^{<1>})T^{<2>}
and
Q_{T}^{-1}=T^{-<1>}S(T^{-<2>})
.
The twisted Hopf algebra
W^{T}
is an algebra with the same algebra structure and the same counit as that of
W
, and the comultiplication and antipode are given by

\Delta^{T}(g)=T^{-1}\Delta(g)T,\quad S^{T}(g)=Q^{-1}_{T}S(g)Q_{T},\quad g\in W.

(8.6)

If
W
is quasitriangular with
R
-matrix
R
, then
W^{T}
is quasitriangular with
R
-matrix
R^{T}=T_{21}^{-1}RT
.
The Haar integral remains unchanged under the twist deformation
Aljadeff et al. 2002
.

Correspondence of boundaries under duality.
—
The boundary theory is determined by a Lagrangian algebra
Kong 2014
, and it can be proved that the duality maps Lagrangian algebra to Lagrangian algebra.
To be precise, if
L
is a Lagrangian algebra of
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))
, then
F(L)
is a Lagrangian algebra of
\mathsf{Rep}(D(W^{\prime}))
, where
F:\mathsf{Rep}(D(W))\to\mathsf{Rep}(D(W^{\prime}))
is a duality, namely,
F
is a braided monoidal equivalence.
Since
F(\oplus_{i}X_{i})=\oplus_{i}F(X_{i})
, the quantum dimension of
\dim F(L)=\dim L
. All other structures are preserved by
F
. This implies that, for the surface with boundaries, the bulk theory is mapped to the dual theory, and the boundary theory is mapped to the boundary theory of the dual theory.

8.2
Duality for the boundary and domain wall

For the
1d
boundary and domain wall of the quantum double phase, the duality still means that two different lattice realizations give the same (monoidal equivalent) boundary phase. Notice that the bulk is now fixed, thus it is different from the boundary correspondence induced by the duality of the bulk phase.

Definition 12
.

1. A duality between two gapped boundaries is an equivalence

\Phi:\mathsf{QD}_{\rm bd}(\mathfrak{A};\partial\Sigma)\to\mathsf{QD}_{\rm bd}(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime};\partial\Sigma^{\prime}),

(8.7)

which consists of (i) a monoidal equivalence between two boundary phases
F:{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}\to{{}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}
; and (ii) a lattice Fourier transform
U_{F}
.

2. For domain wall, a similar definition exists. We can also use the folding trick to transform the domain wall into a boundary and invoke the definition of duality for the boundary to give that to the domain wall.

Therefore, we need firstly to investigate the monoidal equivalent
F:{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}\to{{}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}
.
First notice that
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}}}\textsf{Mod}
. Then using the Eilenberg-Watts theorem
Eilenberg 1960
;
Watts 1960
,
F:{{}_{\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}}}\textsf{Mod}\to{{}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}^{\rm op}}}\textsf{Mod}
is determined by a
{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}^{\rm op}}|{\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}}
-bimodule
M
.
Notice that Morita equivalence of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is a stronger condition, in this case
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}}\textsf{Mod}
.
In a more general TQFT formalism of boundary, this boundary duality between two
\mathcal{C}
-module categories
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})\simeq\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N})
. But the Morita equivalence means that
\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathcal{N}
.
We conjecture that the duality for the boundary can also be characterized by twist deformation of two
W
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
.
Namely,
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{\rm op}}}\textsf{Mod}
is monoidal equivalent to
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}_{2}\otimes\mathfrak{A}_{2}^{\rm op}}}\textsf{Mod}
if and only if
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
are twist equivalent.
We will leave this for our future study.

We would like to stress that the concept of duality given above can be relaxed to
weak duality
.
By which we mean, any two
nd
topological order
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are weakly dual to each other if they can be embedded into gapped boundaries for an
(n+1)d
topological order.
This more general definition of duality allows us to subsume more existing dualities in one unified framework. For example,
the well-known Kramers-Wannier duality between the low-temperature and high-temperature phases of the Ising model can be understood in this way
Freed and Teleman 2022
;
Aasen et al. 2016
;
Aasen et al. 2020
.

9
Conclusion and discussion

In this paper, we investigated the weak Hopf symmetry and weak Hopf quantum double model in detail.
We established the relationship between the weak Hopf quotient algebras and sub-algebras in
W
and
\hat{W}
.
Using this correspondence, we presented a theory of weak Hopf symmetry for vacuum states and its breaking during anyon condensation.
We constructed the weak Hopf quantum double model in
2d
lattice, which has weak Hopf symmetry.
The local stabilizer, ribbon operators, and topological excitations were discussed.
The algebraic theory of gapped boundary and gapped domain wall was established based on comodule algebras and bicomodule algebras.
The lattice construction based on symmetric separability idempotent was given.
To solve the weak Hopf quantum double model, we introduced the weak Hopf tensor network. Then using this representation, we presented the exact solution of the model.
The theory of duality between different weak Hopf quantum double models was also established.

Our work is necessarily technical due to the complication of dealing with weak Hopf algebras.
While some progress has been made, there is much left to be done, and we outline some potentially interesting directions below.

Bulk and boundary twist defects.
—
In our work, we mainly focused on
1d
defects, such as boundaries and domain walls.
We briefly discuss the algebraic classification of the boundary defects and domain wall twist defects via the bimodule category.
A lattice construction of these point defects is an interesting and crucial topic.
Typical examples are Abelian toric codes, wherein the domain wall twist defects have the same statistics as Ising anyons
Bombin 2010
;
Jia and Kaszlikowski 2022
.
And the boundary defects of the Abelian toric code are Majorana fermionic and parafermionic zero modes
Cong et al. 2017b
.
This phenomenon of emerging non-Abelian anyon in the Abelian phase is crucial for both theoretical investigation and applications in topological quantum computing.
For general Hopf and weak Hopf quantum double the structures of these twist defects are more complicated and largely remain open.

Entanglement perspective.
—
We know that the quantum double models have intrinsic topological order, thus their ground states have long-range entanglement.
An entanglement renormalization approach based on our weak Hopf tensor network representations will be helpful for us to understand the weak Hopf quantum double phase.
On the other hand, entanglement entropy is proven to be sensitive to the existence of topological defects, and understanding the entanglement properties in the presence of a gapped boundary, domain wall, and twist defects is a crucial topic.
We left these for our future research.

Symmetry-enriched quantum double model.
—
The quantum double model has the EM duality (see Sec.
8
), and this EM duality could induce a global
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry that exchanges the electric and magnetic charge of the model.
The quantum double can be enriched by this EM duality symmetry.
Systematic construction of the symmetry-enriched quantum double model for arbitrary group
G
(or more generally, Hopf algebra and weak Hopf algebra) is still an open problem.

Higher dimensional extension.
—
While we have a relatively complete understanding of the
2d
topological phase, the algebraic theory and Hamiltonian theory behind the higher dimensional topological orders are still unclear.
Recently, The
3d

\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
model has been investigated from aspects
Levin and Wen 2005b
;
Hamma et al. 2005
;
Kong et al. 2020
;
Delcamp and Schuch 2021
. The
3d
finite group model and its twisted generalization are discussed in
Moradi and Wen 2015
;
Wan et al. 2015
;
Wang et al. 2018
. However, both the higher dimensional case and the more complicated Hopf algebra case are largely unexplored.

Declarations

Conflict of interest:

All authors certify that there are no conflicts of interest for this work.

Data Availability Statement:

Data sharing is not applicable to this article as no datasets were generated or analyzed during the current study.

Appendix A
Some technical details about quantum double

In this appendix, we will give a detailed proof that the definition of the quantum double
D(W)
of a weak Hopf algebra
W
is well defined. This has been shown in Ref.
Nikshych et al. 2003
for an equivalent form of
D(W)
. Nevertheless, it is still worth proving the assertion for the form we used in this work.

Let
J
be the linear span in
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
of the elements

\displaystyle\varphi\otimes xh-\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h,\quad x\in W_{L},

(A.1)

\displaystyle\varphi\otimes yh-\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h,\quad y\in W_{R}.

(A.2)

Then
J
is a two-sided ideal of
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
. To prove this claim, clearly,
J
is an additive subgroup of
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
. For any
\psi\otimes g\in\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
and
x\in W_{L}
, one has

\displaystyle(\psi\otimes g)(\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g),(\varphi)}\psi\varphi^{(2)}\otimes g^{(2)}h\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)}(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon),g^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g),(\varphi)}\psi\varphi^{(2)}\otimes\varepsilon(g^{(2)}x)g^{(3)}h\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(4)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},g^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g),(\varphi)}\psi\varphi^{(2)}\otimes g^{(2)}xh\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},g^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=(\psi\otimes g)(\varphi\otimes xh),

\displaystyle(\varphi\otimes xh)(\psi\otimes g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\varphi\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}g\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},xh^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\varphi\psi^{(2)}\langle\psi^{(3)},x\rangle\otimes h^{(2)}g\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(4)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}g\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=(\varphi(x\rightharpoonup x)\otimes h)(\psi\otimes g).

Similarly, one has

\displaystyle(\psi\otimes g)(\varphi\otimes yh)

\displaystyle=(\psi\otimes g)(\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h),

\displaystyle(\varphi\otimes yh)(\psi\otimes g)

\displaystyle=(\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h)(\psi\otimes g).

The above computation shows that for
x\in J
, we have
(\psi\otimes g)x=x(\psi\otimes g)=0
, so
J
is a two-sided ideal.

The quotient algebra
D(W):=(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)/J
is the quantum double of
W
. We will verify in the following that
D(W)
is a weak Hopf algebra, whose weak Hopf algebra structure is given in Definition
3
.

(i)
D(W)
is an algebra. Associativity: for
\varphi,\psi,\theta\in\hat{W}
and
g,h,k\in W
,

\displaystyle\quad([\varphi\otimes h][\psi\otimes g])[\theta\otimes k]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}[\varphi\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}g][\theta\otimes k]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(g),(\theta)}[(\varphi\psi^{(2)})\theta^{(2)}\otimes(h^{(3)}g^{(2)})k]\langle\theta^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(4)}g^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\theta^{(3)},h^{(2)}g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(g),(\theta)}[\varphi(\psi^{(2)}\theta^{(3)})\otimes h^{(3)}(g^{(2)}k)]\langle\theta^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\rangle\langle\theta^{(2)},S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\theta^{(4)},h^{(2)}\rangle\langle\theta^{(5)},g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g),(\theta)}\sum_{(h),(\psi)}[\varphi(\psi^{(2)}\theta^{(3)})\otimes h^{(2)}(g^{(2)}k)]\langle\psi^{(1)}\theta^{(2)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)}\theta^{(4)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\theta^{(5)},g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\theta^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g),(\theta)}[\varphi\otimes h][\psi\theta^{(2)}\otimes g^{(2)}k]\langle\theta^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\rangle\langle\theta^{(3)},g^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=[\varphi\otimes h]([\psi\otimes g][\theta\otimes k]).

Unit property: since
\Delta(1_{W})\in W_{R}\otimes W_{L}
,

\displaystyle[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}][\varphi\otimes h]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\sum_{(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}h]\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\sum_{(1^{\prime}_{W})}\sum_{(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes\langle\varphi^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle 1_{W}^{(2)}1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(2)})\rangle h]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\sum_{(1^{\prime}_{W})}\sum_{(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}(1_{W}^{(2)}\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\langle\varphi^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup 1_{W}^{\prime(1)})\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(2)})\rangle\otimes h]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi^{(3)})S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi^{(1)}))\otimes h]=[\varphi\otimes h].

Similarly,
[\varphi\otimes h][\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}]=[\varphi\otimes h]
.

(ii)
\Delta,~\varepsilon
and
S
are well defined. First,
\Delta
is well defined:

\displaystyle\Delta([\varphi\otimes xh])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes xh^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\Delta([\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h]),

\displaystyle\Delta([\varphi\otimes yh])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes yh^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\Delta([\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h]).

Next,
\varepsilon
is well defined:

\displaystyle\varepsilon([\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon),\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h))1_{W}^{(2)}x\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon,1_{W}^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))S^{-1}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon,1_{W}^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(xh))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(xh))1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi,\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(xh))\rangle=\varepsilon([\varphi\otimes xh]),

\displaystyle\varepsilon([\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y),\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon,y\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h))1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi,yS^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))\rangle=\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h)S(y))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(yh))\rangle=\varepsilon([\varphi\otimes yh]),

where we used

\displaystyle x\varepsilon_{L}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}xh^{(1)}S(h^{(2)})=\sum_{(xh)}(xh)^{(1)}S((xh)^{(2)})=\varepsilon_{L}(xh),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{L}(h)S(

\displaystyle y)=\sum_{(h)}h^{(1)}S(yh^{(2)})=\sum_{(yh)}(yh)^{(1)}S((yh)^{(2)})=\varepsilon_{L}(yh).

Lastly,
S
is well defined:

\displaystyle S([\varphi\otimes xh])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(1)},h^{(3)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},S^{-1}(xh^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(1)},h^{(3)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)}(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon),S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=S([\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h]),

\displaystyle S([\varphi\otimes yh])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(1)},yh^{(3)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(1)}(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y),h^{(3)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=S([\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)\otimes h]).

(iii)
\Delta
is coassociative and multiplicative:

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})([\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\varphi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(3)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)([\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}])

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta([\varphi\otimes h]),

\displaystyle\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])\Delta([\psi\otimes g])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}][\psi^{(2)}\otimes g^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}][\psi^{(1)}\otimes g^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\psi^{(5)}\otimes h^{(2)}g^{(1)}]\langle\psi^{(4)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(6)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(5)}g^{(2)}]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(6)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(4)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\psi^{(4)}\otimes h^{(2)}g^{(1)}]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(5)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(4)}g^{(2)}]\langle\psi^{(3)},S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\psi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(2)}g^{(1)}]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(4)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}(S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)}))\rightharpoonup\psi^{(2)})\otimes h^{(4)}g^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\psi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(2)}g^{(1)}]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(4)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\psi^{(2)}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)})\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h^{(4)}g^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\psi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(2)}g^{(1)}]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(4)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\psi^{(2)}\otimes\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)})h^{(4)}g^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(\psi),(g)}[\varphi^{(2)}\psi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(2)}g^{(1)}]\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(4)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\otimes[\varphi^{(1)}\psi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(3)}g^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\Delta([\varphi\otimes h][\psi\otimes g]).

(iv) The counit property holds:

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\langle\varphi^{(2)},\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(1)}))\rangle[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\,[S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)}))\rightharpoonup\varphi)\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\,[\varphi(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\,[\varphi\otimes\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(1)})h^{(2)}]=[\varphi\otimes h],

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\,\varepsilon)\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]\langle\varphi^{(1)},\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(2)}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}[(\varphi\leftharpoonup\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(2)})))\otimes h^{(1)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}[\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(2)})))\otimes h^{(1)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}[\varphi\otimes\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(h^{(2)}))h^{(1)}]=[\varphi\otimes h].

(v) Weak multiplicativity of the counit:

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon([\varphi\otimes h][\psi\otimes g][\theta\otimes k])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(g),(\theta)}\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)}\theta^{(2)})),h^{(3)}g^{(2)}k\rangle\langle\theta^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(4)}g^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\theta^{(3)},h^{(2)}g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(g),(\theta)}\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\langle\varepsilon^{(1)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)}\theta^{(2)})),h^{(3)}g^{(2)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},k\rangle\langle S^{-1}(\theta^{(1)}),h^{(4)}g^{(3)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\theta^{(3)},h^{(2)}g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(5)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(\theta)}\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\langle\theta^{(3)}\varepsilon^{(1)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)}\theta^{(2)}))\hat{S}^{-1}(\theta^{(1)}),h^{(2)}g\rangle\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},k\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(\theta\varepsilon^{(1)}))\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)})),h^{(2)}g\rangle\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},k\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(g)}\sum_{(\varepsilon),(\varepsilon^{\prime})}\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(\theta\varepsilon^{(1)}))\varepsilon^{\prime(2)},g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)})),h^{(2)}g^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},k\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)}\varepsilon^{\prime(1)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(\theta),(g)}\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\langle\theta^{(3)}\varepsilon^{(1)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\psi^{(4)}\theta^{(2)}))\hat{S}^{-1}(\theta^{(1)}),g^{(1)}\rangle\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)})),h^{(2)}g^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},k\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\psi)}\sum_{(\theta),(g)}\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi\psi^{(2)})),h^{(2)}g^{(4)}\rangle\langle\psi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle\psi^{(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\psi^{(4)}\theta^{(2)})),g^{(2)}k\rangle\langle\theta^{(1)},S^{-1}(g^{(3)})\rangle\langle\theta^{(3)},g^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\psi),(g)}\varepsilon([\varphi\otimes h][\psi^{(1)}\otimes g^{(2)}])\varepsilon([\psi^{(2)}\otimes g^{(1)}][\theta\otimes k]).

Similarly, one can show the other identity for weak multiplicativity of the counit.

(vi) Weak comultiplicativity of the unit:

\displaystyle\quad(\Delta([\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}])\otimes[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}])([\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}]\otimes\Delta([\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}]))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(1^{\prime}_{W}),(\varepsilon^{\prime})}[\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}]\otimes[\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}][\varepsilon^{\prime(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{\prime(1)}]\otimes[\varepsilon^{\prime(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{\prime(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(1^{\prime}_{W}),(\varepsilon^{\prime})}[\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}]\otimes[\varepsilon^{(1)}\varepsilon^{\prime(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}1_{W}^{\prime(1)}]\otimes[\varepsilon^{\prime(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{\prime(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}[\varepsilon^{(3)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}]\otimes[\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}]\otimes[\varepsilon^{(1)},1_{W}^{(3)}]

\displaystyle=((\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta)([\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}]).

Similarly, one can show the other identity for weak comultiplicativity of the unit.

(vii) The above computation shows that
D(W)
is a weak bialgebra. In order to prove it is a weak Hopf algebra, let us compute the left counit:

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon_{L}([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\varepsilon([\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}][\varphi\otimes h])[\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(\varphi)}\langle\varepsilon^{(2)}\varphi^{(2)},\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)}h))\rangle\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle[\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(4)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(2)},\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)}h))1_{W}^{\prime(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(3)},1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle[\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(4)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\rangle\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(3)})\varepsilon_{R}(S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)}h))1_{W}^{\prime(2)}1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle[\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(4)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\rangle\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(1_{W}^{(3)}))S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)})1_{W}^{\prime(2)}1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle[\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes 1_{W}^{(4)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))S^{-1}(S(1_{W}^{\prime(2)})1_{W}^{(2)})1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle[\varepsilon\otimes S(1_{W}^{\prime(1)})1_{W}^{(3)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)})1_{W}^{(1)}\rangle[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}^{(3)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W})}\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(1)})\rangle[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}].

(A.3)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}]S([\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\varphi^{(6)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(3)})\otimes h^{(2)}S(h^{(5)})]\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(4)}),S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)}),h^{(1)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(1)},h^{(6)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(5)},S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\varphi^{(4)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes h^{(2)}S(h^{(4)})]\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(3)}),S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)}))\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varphi^{(1)},h^{(5)}S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(1_{W})}[\varphi^{(4)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})]\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(3)}),S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)}S(h^{(3)}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W}),(1_{W}^{\prime})}[\varphi^{(4)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}1_{W}^{\prime(2)}]\langle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(3)}),S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\varepsilon_{L}(h))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W}),(1_{W}^{\prime})}[\varphi^{(3)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(2)}))\otimes 1_{W}^{(1)}1_{W}^{\prime(2)}]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\varepsilon_{L}(h))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}[\varphi^{(3)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes 1_{W}^{\prime(2)}]\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\varepsilon_{L}(h))\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1_{W}^{\prime})}\langle\varphi,S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h))S^{-1}(1_{W}^{\prime(1)})\rangle[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}^{\prime(2)}]=\varepsilon_{L}([\varphi\otimes h]).

Similarly, one can show that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{R}([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\langle\varepsilon^{(1)}\hat{S}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi)),h\rangle[\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes 1_{W}],

(A.4)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{R}([\varphi\otimes

\displaystyle h])=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}S([\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}])[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}].

(A.5)

Finally, we compute

\displaystyle\quad\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(S\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes S)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(S\otimes\varepsilon_{L})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}S([\varphi^{(2)}\otimes h^{(1)}])\varepsilon_{L}([\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(1_{W})}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(3)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(2)},h^{(3)}\rangle\langle\varphi^{(4)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(4)}))S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(1)})\rangle[\varepsilon\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}\sum_{(1_{W})}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})1_{W}^{(2)}]\langle\varphi^{(3)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(1)},S^{-1}(1_{W}^{(1)})h^{(3)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varphi),(h)}[\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)})]\langle\varphi^{(3)},S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(1)},h^{(3)}\rangle=S([\varphi\otimes h]),

as desired. Hence we finish the proof that
D(W)
is a weak Hopf algebra.

The following result has been shown in
Majid 2000
for Hopf algebras and in
Nikshych et al. 2003
for weak Hopf algebras (note that they used a different (yet equivalent) form of the quantum double). We include proof for the form of the quantum double we used here for completeness.

Proposition 6
.

Let
W
be a weak Hopf algebra. Then the quantum double
D(W)
in Definition
3
has a quasitriangular structure given by

R=\sum_{i}[\varepsilon\otimes x_{i}]\otimes[x^{i}\otimes 1_{W}],\quad\tilde{R}=\sum_{j}[\varepsilon\otimes S(x_{j})]\otimes[x^{j}\otimes 1_{W}],

(A.6)

where
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{x^{i}\}
are dual bases of
W
and
\hat{W}
.

Proof.

With the identifications
W\simeq(\varepsilon\otimes W)/J
and
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\simeq(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes 1_{W})/J
, the identities
(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(R)=R_{13}R_{12}
and
(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(R)=R_{13}R_{23}
are equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{(x^{i})}x_{i}\otimes x^{i,(2)}\otimes x^{i,(1)}=\sum_{i,j}x_{i}x_{j}\otimes x^{j}\otimes x^{i},

(A.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{(x_{i})}x_{i}^{(1)}\otimes x_{i}^{(2)}\otimes x^{i}=\sum_{i,j}x_{i}\otimes x_{j}\otimes x^{i}x^{j}.

(A.8)

We prove the first one and the second one is similarly proved. To this end, by evaluating both sides on an element
\varphi\in\hat{W}
in the first factor, we have

\displaystyle\text{LHS}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{(x^{i})}\langle\varphi,x_{i}\rangle x^{i,(2)}\otimes x^{i,(1)}=\sum_{i}\Delta^{\rm op}(\langle\varphi,x_{i}\rangle x^{i})=\Delta^{\rm op}(\varphi),

(A.9)

\displaystyle\text{RHS}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\langle\varphi,x_{i}x_{j}\rangle x^{j}\otimes x^{i}=\sum_{i,j}\sum_{(\varphi)}\langle\varphi^{(2)},x_{j}\rangle x^{j}\otimes\langle\varphi^{(1)},x_{i}\rangle x^{i}=\Delta^{\rm op}(\varphi),

where we have used
\varphi=\sum_{i}\langle\varphi,x_{i}\rangle x^{i}
. By evaluating both sides on general elements
a\otimes b\in W\otimes W
in the second and third factors also gives the same result, hence Eq. (
A.7
) holds. Now we show
\Delta^{\rm op}([\varphi\otimes h])R=R\Delta([\varphi\otimes h])
for all
[\varphi\otimes h]\in D(W)
. In fact, using
h=\sum_{i}\langle x^{i},h\rangle x_{i}
and
\varphi=\sum_{i}\langle\varphi,x_{i}\rangle x^{i}
, we get

\displaystyle\quad R(\Delta[\varphi\otimes h])

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,(x_{i})}\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}[\varphi^{(3)}\otimes x_{i}^{(2)}h^{(1)}]\otimes[x^{i}\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]\langle\varphi^{(2)},S^{-1}(x_{i}^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(4)},x_{i}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,(x_{i})}\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(3)}\otimes\langle x^{j},x_{i}^{(2)}\rangle x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[x^{i}\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]\langle\varphi^{(2)},S^{-1}(x_{i}^{(3)})\rangle\langle\varphi^{(4)},x_{i}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(3)}\otimes x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(4)}x^{j}S^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W})}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(3)}x^{j}(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup 1_{W}^{(1)})\langle S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi^{(1)})),1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W})}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(2)}\otimes x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(3)}x^{j}\otimes\langle S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi^{(1)})),1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle 1_{W}^{(1)}h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{(1_{W}),(\varepsilon)}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(1)}\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}x^{j}\otimes\langle S^{-1}(\varepsilon^{(1)}),1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle 1_{W}^{(1)}h^{(2)}]

(A.10)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(1)}\varepsilon^{(2)}\otimes x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}x^{j}\otimes\langle S^{-1}(\varepsilon^{(1)}),\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)})\rangle h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(1)}(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(3)})))\otimes x_{j}h^{(1)}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}x^{j}\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{j}\sum_{i}\,[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(4)}\langle x^{i},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})x_{j}h^{(1)}\rangle x_{i}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}x^{j}\otimes h^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{i,(x^{i})}\sum_{j}\,[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(4)}x_{i}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}\langle x^{i,(2)},x_{j}\rangle x^{j}\otimes h^{(2)}]\langle x^{i,(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle x^{i,(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\sum_{i,(x^{i})}[\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(4)}x_{i}]\otimes[\varphi^{(2)}x^{i,(2)}\otimes h^{(2)}]\langle x^{i,(1)},S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\rangle\langle x^{i,(3)},h^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\rm op}([\varphi\otimes h])R,

where in the sixth equality, we used
\sum_{(\varphi)}\varepsilon_{R}(\varphi^{(1)})\otimes\varphi^{(2)}=\sum_{(\varepsilon)}\varepsilon^{(1)}\otimes\varphi\varepsilon^{(2)}
; in the seventh equality, we used
\sum_{(h)}h^{(1)}\otimes\varepsilon_{L}(h^{(2)})=\sum_{(1_{W})}1_{W}^{(1)}h\otimes 1_{W}^{(2)}
. It remains to show that the element
\tilde{R}
satisfies
\tilde{R}R=\Delta(1_{D(W)})
and
R\tilde{R}=\Delta^{\rm op}(1_{D(W)})
. The first identity is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\,[\varepsilon\otimes S(x_{i})x_{j}]\otimes[x^{i}x^{j}\otimes 1_{W}]

(A.11)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varepsilon),(1_{W})}\sum_{(\varepsilon^{\prime}),(1^{\prime}_{W})}[\varepsilon\otimes\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},1_{W}^{\prime(2)}\rangle 1_{W}^{\prime(1)}1_{W}^{(1)}]\otimes[\langle\varepsilon^{\prime(2)},1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle\varepsilon^{(1)}\varepsilon^{\prime(1)}\otimes 1_{W}],

which is equivalent to

\quad\sum_{i,j}\,x^{i}x^{j}\otimes S(x_{i})x_{j}=\sum_{(\varepsilon),(1_{W})}\sum_{(\varepsilon^{\prime}),(1^{\prime}_{W})}\langle\varepsilon^{\prime(2)},1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle\varepsilon^{(1)}\varepsilon^{\prime(1)}\otimes\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},1_{W}^{\prime(2)}\rangle 1_{W}^{\prime(1)}1_{W}^{(1)}

(A.12)

when regarded as an equality in
\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
. To show this equality, evaluating both sides for a general
h\in W
in the first factor, one has

RHS

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\varepsilon),(1_{W})}\sum_{(\varepsilon^{\prime}),(1^{\prime}_{W})}\langle\varepsilon^{\prime(2)},1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle\langle\varepsilon^{(1)}\varepsilon^{\prime(1)},h\rangle\langle\varepsilon^{(2)},1_{W}^{\prime(2)}\rangle 1_{W}^{\prime(1)}1_{W}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(1^{\prime}_{W})}1_{W}^{\prime(1)}\langle\varepsilon,h^{(1)}1_{W}^{\prime(2)}\rangle\sum_{(1_{W})}1_{W}^{(1)}\langle\varepsilon^{\prime},h^{(2)}1_{W}^{(2)}\rangle

(A.13)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(1)})\varepsilon_{R}(h^{(2)})=\sum_{(h)}S(h^{(1)})h^{(2)}=\text{LHS}.

When evaluating both sides for a general element
\varphi\in\hat{W}
in the second factor, the results are still the same. Thus Eq. (
A.12
) holds. The second identity is proved similarly.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Lemma
4
and Lemma
5

Proof of Lemma
4
.

First note that the dual space
D(W)^{\vee}
can be identified with a subspace of
(\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W)^{\vee}\simeq W^{\rm op}\otimes\hat{W}
consisting of elements
g\otimes\psi\in W^{\rm op}\otimes\hat{W}
which vanish on the ideal
J\subset\hat{W}^{\rm cop}\otimes W
, i.e.,
g\otimes\psi\in D(W)^{\vee}
if and only if

\varphi(g)\psi(xh)=\varphi(x\rightharpoonup\varepsilon)(g)\psi(h),\quad\varphi(g)\psi(yh)=\varphi(\varepsilon\leftharpoonup y)(g)\psi(h),

(B.1)

for any
x\in W_{L},\,y\in W_{R}
and
h\in W,\,\varphi\in\hat{W}
.

First consider the case
\rho=\tau_{R}^{+}
:

x_{4}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{1}
s
x_{5}
x_{6}
F^{g,\psi}(\tau_{R}^{+})|x_{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)T_{-}^{\psi}|x_{1}\rangle

In this case, one has

\displaystyle\quad F^{xg,\psi}(\rho)A^{h}(s)|x_{4},x_{5},x_{6},x_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{1})}F^{xg,\psi}(\rho)|h^{(1)}x_{4},x_{5}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{6},x_{1}S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{1})}\varepsilon(xg)\psi(S(x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)})))|h^{(1)}x_{4},x_{5}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{6},x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{1})}\varepsilon(S(x)g)\psi(S(x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)})))|h^{(1)}x_{4},x_{5}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{6},x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{(x_{1})}\varepsilon(g)\psi(S(x_{1}^{(1)}S^{-1}(h^{(5)})x))|h^{(1)}x_{4},x_{5}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{6},x_{1}^{(2)}S^{-1}(h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{g,\psi}(\rho)|h^{(1)}x_{4},x_{5}S^{-1}(h^{(2)}),h^{(3)}x_{6},x_{1}S^{-1}(S(x)h^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)A^{S(x)h}(s)|x_{4},x_{5},x_{6},x_{1}\rangle,

and

\displaystyle\quad F^{g,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi\leftharpoonup x}(s)|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{i})}F^{g,\psi}(\rho)\varphi(xS(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})S(x_{3}^{(1)})S(x_{4}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(2)},x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(2)},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{i})}F^{g,\psi}(\rho)\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})S(x_{3}^{(1)})S(x_{4}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(2)}x,x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(2)},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{i})}\varepsilon(g)\psi(S(x_{1}^{(2)}x))\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})S(x_{3}^{(1)})S(x_{4}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(3)},x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(2)},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{i})}\varepsilon(S(x)g)\psi(S(x_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})S(x_{3}^{(1)})S(x_{4}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(3)},x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(2)},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{i})}\varepsilon(xg)\psi(S(x_{1}^{(2)}))\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})S(x_{3}^{(1)})S(x_{4}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(3)},x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(2)},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{i})}F^{xg,\psi}(\rho)\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})S(x_{3}^{(1)})S(x_{4}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(2)},x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(2)},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=F^{xg,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi}(s)|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle.

The general cases follow from the recursive formula of ribbon operators:

\displaystyle F^{xg,\varphi}(\rho)A^{h}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum F^{xg^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{h}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum F^{xg^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})A^{h}(s)F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})A^{S(x)h}(s)F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})A^{S(x)h}(s)

\displaystyle=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)A^{S(x)h}(s).

The other identity is verified similarly. Thus this finishes the proof of Lemma
4
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Although we are in the context of weak Hopf algebras, the proof of Lemma
5
is essentially the same as that in Ref.
Yan et al. 2022
. Following the technique there, we will only prove the identities (
4.42
) and (
4.43
) for an example to show how to deal with the computation in our setting.

\bullet
(
4.42
) for short ribbons:

x_{4}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{1}
s_{0}
\rho=\tau_{R}\cup\tilde{\tau}_{L}:

\displaystyle\quad B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(h)}B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\tau_{R})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}({\tilde{\tau}_{L}})|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(h)}B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\tau_{R})\varepsilon(\varphi(k^{(2)})\bullet)|x_{1},x_{2}S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(h)}\sum_{(x_{1})}B^{\psi}(s_{0})\varepsilon(h^{(1)})\varepsilon(\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet))\hat{k}(S(x_{1}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(2)},x_{2}S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k)}\sum_{(x_{1})}B^{\psi}(s_{0})\varphi(k^{(2)})\hat{k}(S(x_{1}^{(1)}))|x_{1}^{(2)},x_{2}S^{-1}(k^{(3)})hk^{(1)},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{1})}B^{\psi}(s_{0})\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(2)}))|x_{1}^{(4)},x_{2}x_{1}^{(1)}hS(x_{1}^{(3)}),x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(S(x_{1}^{(6)})S(x_{2}^{(1)}x_{1}^{(1)}h^{(1)}S(x_{1}^{(5)}))x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|x_{1}^{(7)},x_{2}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}S(x_{1}^{(4)}),x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(S(\varepsilon_{R}(x_{1}^{(5)}))S(h^{(1)})S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|x_{1}^{(6)},x_{2}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}S(x_{1}^{(4)}),x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(S(h^{(1)})S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|x_{1}^{(6)},x_{2}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}S(\varepsilon_{R}(x_{1}^{(5)}))S(x_{1}^{(4)}),x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\varphi(S(x_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(S(h^{(1)})S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|x_{1}^{(5)},x_{2}^{(2)}x_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}S(x_{1}^{(4)}),x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\sum_{k,(k)}\varepsilon(h^{(2)})\varphi(k^{(2)})\hat{k}(S(x_{1}^{(2)}))\psi(S(h^{(1)})S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|x_{1}^{(3)},x_{2}^{(2)}S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)},x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\sum_{k,(k)}F^{h^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\tau_{R})\varphi(k^{(2)})\psi(S(h^{(1)})S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|x_{1}^{(2)},x_{2}^{(2)}S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)},x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h),(x_{i})}\sum_{k,(k)}F^{h^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\tau_{R})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\tilde{\tau}_{L})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\psi(S(h^{(1)})S(x_{1}^{(1)})S(x_{2}^{(1)})x_{3}^{(2)}x_{4}^{(2)})|x_{1}^{(2)},x_{2}^{(2)},x_{3}^{(1)},x_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\varphi}(\rho)B^{\psi(S(h^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{0})|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle.

4.42
) for long ribbons:

\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
\rho_{1}=\tau_{R}

\displaystyle\quad B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})B^{\psi(S(h^{(1)})\bullet)}(s_{0})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h^{(2)})}F^{h^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\psi(S(h^{(1)})\bullet)}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\varphi}(\rho)B^{\psi(S(h^{(1)})\bullet)}(s_{0}).

\rho=\tau_{L}:

\displaystyle\quad A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\tau_{L})|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{4})}A^{g}(s_{1})\varepsilon(h)\varphi(x_{4}^{(1)})|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{4})}\sum_{(g)}\varepsilon(h)\varphi(x_{4}^{(1)})|g^{(1)}x_{1},x_{2}S^{-1}(g^{(2)}),g^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(g^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{4})}\sum_{(g)}\varepsilon(h)\varphi(x_{4}^{(1)})|g^{(1)}x_{1},x_{2}S^{-1}(g^{(2)}),g^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(\varepsilon_{R}(g^{(5)}))S^{-1}(g^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{4})}\sum_{(g)}\varepsilon(h)\varphi(x_{4}^{(1)}S^{-2}(\varepsilon_{R}(g^{(5)})))|g^{(1)}x_{1},x_{2}S^{-1}(g^{(2)}),g^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(g^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{4})}\sum_{(g)}\varepsilon(h)\varphi(x_{4}^{(1)}S^{-1}(g^{(5)})S^{-2}(g^{(6)}))|g^{(1)}x_{1},x_{2}S^{-1}(g^{(2)}),g^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}^{(2)}S^{-1}(g^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet S^{-2}(g^{(5)}))}(\tau_{L})|g^{(1)}x_{1},x_{2}S^{-1}(g^{(2)}),g^{(3)}x_{3},x_{4}S^{-1}(g^{(4)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet S^{-2}(g^{(2)}))}(\tau_{L})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{1})|x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle.

\displaystyle\quad A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})A^{g}(s_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S^{-1}(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet S^{-2}(g^{(2)}))}(\rho_{2})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet S^{-2}(g^{(2)}))}(\rho_{A})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{1}).

The other identities can be proved with the same technique. ∎
